On a local geometric property of the generalized elastic transmission eigenfunctions and application
Huaian Diao, Hongyu Liu, Baiyi Sun

Abstract
. Consider the nonlinear and completely continuous scattering map 𝒮 ⁡ ( ( Ω , λ , μ , V ) , 𝐮 i ) = 𝐮 t ∞ ​ ( 𝐱 ^ ) , 𝐱 ^ ∈ 𝕊 n − 1 , \mathcal{S}\big((\Omega;\lambda,\mu,V),\mathbf{u}^{i}\big)=\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}),\quad\hat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}, which sends an inhomogeneous elastic scatterer ( Ω , λ , μ , V ) (\Omega;\lambda,\mu,V) to its far-field pattern 𝐮 t ∞ \mathbf{u}_{t}^{\infty} due to an incident wave field 𝐮 i \mathbf{u}^{i} via the Lamé system. Here, ( λ , μ , V ) (\lambda,\mu,V) signifies the medium configuration of an elastic scatterer that is compactly supported in Ω \Omega . In this paper, we are concerned with the intrinsic geometric structure of the kernel space of 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , which is of fundamental importance to the theory of inverse scattering and invisibility cloaking for elastic waves and has received considerable attention recently. It turns out that the study is contained in analysing the geometric properties of a certain non-selfadjoint and non-elliptic transmission eigenvalue problem. We propose a generalized elastic transmission eigenvalue problem and prove that the transmission eigenfunctions vanish locally around a corner of ∂ Ω \partial\Omega under generic regularity criteria. The regularity criteria are characerized by the Hölder continuity or a certain Fourier extension property of the transmission eigenfunctions. As an interesting and significant application, we apply the local geometric property to derive several novel unique identifiability results for a longstanding inverse elastic problem by a single far-field measurement. Keywords: Elasticity, non-scattering and invisibility, transmission eigenfunctions, geometric structure, corner singularity, inverse obstacle problem, unique identifiability, single far-field pattern. AMS Class (2010): 35Q60, 78A46 (primary); 35P25, 78A05, 81U40 (secondary).



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and motivation

We first introduce the elastic scattering due to an embedded inhomogeneous medium and an incident wave field, which is the physical origin of our study. Let
\lambda,\mu
be real constants satisfying the following strong convexity condition

\mu>0,\ \ n\lambda+2\mu>0\ \mbox{ for }\ n=2,3.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n=2,3
, be a bounded Lipschitz domain with a connected complement
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\overline{\Omega}
. Suppose that
V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a real-valued function with
\mathrm{supp}(V)\subset\Omega
. The parameters
\lambda,\mu
and
V
characterize the elastic medium configuration of the space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with
\lambda,\mu
and
1+V
respectively denoting the bulk moduli and density. Throughout, we assume that
V
is nontrivial, which is also referred to as a scattering potential.
(\Omega;\lambda,\mu,V)
signifies an inhomogeneous scatterer embedded in the uniformly homogeneous space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
\mathbf{u}^{i}
be an incident field which is a
\mathbb{C}^{n}
-valued entire solution to the following Lamé system:

(1.1)

\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}^{i}+\omega^{2}\mathbf{u}^{i}=0\ \ \mbox{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}^{i}:=\lambda\Delta\mathbf{u}^{i}+(\lambda+\mu)\nabla\nabla\cdot{\mathbf{u}^{i}},

where
\omega\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
signifies the angular frequency of the time-harmonic wave propagation. The interaction between the incident field
\mathbf{u}^{i}
and the scatterer
(\Omega;\lambda,\mu,V)
generates the elastic scattering, which is governed by the following Lamé system:

(1.2)

\mathcal{L}{\mathbf{u}}+\omega^{2}(1+V)\mathbf{u}=0\ \ \mbox{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{n};\ \ \mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{i}+\mathbf{u}^{\mathrm{sc}};\ \ \mathbf{u}^{\mathrm{sc}}\ \mbox{is radiating}.

Here by radiating, we mean that
\mathbf{u}^{\mathrm{sc}}
satisfies the following Kupradze radiation condition

(1.3)

\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}r^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\left(\frac{\partial{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{\mathrm{sc}}}{\partial r}-\mathrm{i}k_{\beta}{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{\mathrm{sc}}\right)=\,\mathbf{0},\quad r:=|\mathbf{x}|,\quad\beta=p,s,

where

(1.4)

{\mathbf{u}}^{\mathrm{sc}}={\mathbf{u}}_{p}^{\mathrm{sc}}+{\mathbf{u}}_{s}^{\mathrm{sc}},\ \ {\mathbf{u}}_{p}^{\mathrm{sc}}:=-\frac{1}{k_{p}^{2}}\nabla\left(\nabla\cdot{\mathbf{u}}^{\mathrm{sc}}\right),\quad{\mathbf{u}}_{s}^{\mathrm{sc}}:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{k_{s}^{2}}\nabla\times\nabla\times{\mathbf{u}}^{\mathrm{sc}}\ \ &(\mbox{3D})\\
\frac{1}{k_{s}^{2}}\bf{curl}\operatorname{curl}u^{\mathrm{sc}}\ \ &(\mbox{2D})\end{cases},

and

(1.5)

k_{p}:=\frac{\omega}{\sqrt{2\mu+\lambda}},\ \ k_{s}:=\frac{\omega}{\sqrt{\mu}}.

In (
1.4
), the two-dimensional operators
\bf{curl}
and
\operatorname{curl}
are defined respectively by

{\rm curl}\,{\mathbf{u}}=\partial_{1}u_{2}-\partial_{2}u_{1},\quad{\bf curl}\,{u}=(\partial_{2}u,-\partial_{1}u)^{\top},

with
{\mathbf{u}}=(u_{1},u_{2})
and
u
being vector-valued and scalar functions, respectively. It is noted that (
1.4
) is the Helmholtz decomposition of the vector field
{\mathbf{u}}^{\mathrm{sc}}
, where
{\mathbf{u}}_{p}^{\mathrm{s}c}
and
{\mathbf{u}}_{s}^{\mathrm{s}c}
are respectively referred to as the compressional and shear parts of
{\mathbf{u}}^{\mathrm{s}c}
. This decomposition shall also play an important role in our subsequent analysis. The Kupradze radiation condition characterizes the outward propagating nature of the scattered field
{\mathbf{u}}^{\mathrm{sc}}
. The well-posedness of the scattering system (
1.2
) is known
[
31
]
, and in particular there exists a unique solution
{\mathbf{u}}\in H_{loc}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which admits the following asymptotic expansions:

(1.6)

\begin{split}{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbf{x}})=&\frac{{e}^{\mathrm{i}k_{p}r}}{{r}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}\left\{u_{p}^{\infty}(\hat{{\mathbf{x}}})\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}+O\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\right\},\ \ \hat{{\mathbf{x}}}:={\mathbf{x}}/|{\mathbf{x}}|,\ \ \beta=p,s,\\
{\mathbf{u}}^{\mathrm{sc}}({\mathbf{x}})=&\frac{{e}^{\mathrm{i}k_{p}r}}{{r}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}u_{p}^{\infty}(\hat{{\mathbf{x}}})\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}+\frac{{e}^{\mathrm{i}k_{s}r}}{{r}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}u_{s}^{\infty}(\hat{{\mathbf{x}}})\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\perp}+O\left(\frac{1}{r^{(n+1)/2}}\right),\end{split}

as
r=|{\mathbf{x}}|\rightarrow\infty
, where
u_{p}^{\infty}
and
u_{s}^{\infty}
are both scalar functions defined on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}:=\{\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\big||\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}|=1\}
. Define the far-field pattern
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}^{\infty}
of
{\mathbf{u}}^{\mathrm{sc}}
as
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}^{\infty}(\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}):=u_{p}^{\infty}(\hat{{\mathbf{x}}})\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}+u_{s}^{\infty}(\hat{{\mathbf{x}}})\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\perp}.
One clearly has the unique correspondences:

{\mathbf{u}}_{p}^{\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})={\mathbf{u}}_{t}^{\infty}(\hat{{\mathbf{x}}})\cdot\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}
and
{\mathbf{u}}_{s}^{\infty}(\hat{{\mathbf{x}}})={\mathbf{u}}_{t}^{\infty}(\hat{{\mathbf{x}}})\cdot\hat{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\perp}
. Moreover, due to the Rellich Theorem
[
20
]
, the correspondence between
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}^{\infty}
and
\mathbf{u}^{\mathrm{s}c}
is also one-to-one.

Define the scattering operator
\mathcal{S}
as

(1.7)

\mathcal{S}\big((\Omega;\lambda,\mu,V),\mathbf{u}^{i}\big)=\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}),\quad\hat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1},

which is implicitly defined by the scattering system (
1.2
). It can be directly verified that
\mathcal{S}
is nonlinear with respect to the argument
(\Omega;\lambda,\mu,V)
, whereas it is linear with respect to the argument
\mathbf{u}^{i}
. An inverse problem of industrial importance in geophysical exploration and medical imaging is to recover
(\Omega;\lambda,\mu,V)
by knowledge of
\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\infty}
. In this paper, we are mainly concerned with the kernel space of
\mathcal{S}
, namely

(1.8)

\mathcal{S}\big((\Omega;\lambda,\mu,V),\mathbf{u}^{i}\big)=\mathbf{0},

which corresponds to the physical scenario that no scattering, a.k.a. invisibility occurs. In particular, we consider the geometric structures of the kernel space
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{S})
, namely, the quantitative relationships between the configurations in
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{S})
and the intrinsic geometries of
\Omega
. Intuitively, if non-scattering/invisibility occurs,
one has nil scattering information from the far-field measurement and hence the inverse problem described above fails. However, it is much surprising that the geometric understanding of
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{S})
can not only provide quantitative understanding of how the waves behave inside the scattering object when invisibility occurs with respect to exterior observations, but can also provide a completely new perspective of the inverse problem (
1.7
) for both the derivation of new theoretical uniqueness results and the development of novel numerical reconstruction algorithms. Before discussing more about these aspects, we present the so-called elastic transmission eigenvalue problem, which shall provide a broader spectral perspective of studying the geometric structure of
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{S})
.

Consider the following PDE system for
{\mathbf{v}}=(v_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{n}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{n}
and
{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{n}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{n}
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\lambda\Delta{\mathbf{v}}+(\lambda+\mu)\nabla\nabla\cdot{\mathbf{v}}+\omega^{2}{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{0}}}\hskip 58.04343pt\ \mbox{ in }\Omega,\\[5.0pt]
{\lambda\Delta{\mathbf{w}}+(\lambda+\mu)\nabla\nabla\cdot{\mathbf{w}}+\omega^{2}(1+V){\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{0}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\ \mbox{ in }\Omega,\\[5.0pt]
{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{v}},\quad T_{\nu}{\mathbf{v}}=T_{\nu}{\mathbf{w}}\hskip 116.65646pt\mbox{ on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

with
\nu
signifying the outward unit normal to
\partial\Omega
, and the
boundary traction
operator
T_{\nu}{\mathbf{v}}
defined as

(1.12)

T_{\nu}{\mathbf{v}}=\begin{cases}2\mu\partial_{\nu}{\mathbf{v}}+\lambda{\nu}\left(\nabla\cdot{\mathbf{v}}\right)+\mu(\partial_{2}v_{1}-\partial_{1}v_{2})\nu^{\perp},\quad\mbox{ for }n=2,\\[5.0pt]
2\mu\partial_{\nu}{\mathbf{v}}+\lambda{\nu}\left(\nabla\cdot{\mathbf{v}}\right)+\mu{\nu}\times(\nabla\times{\mathbf{v}}),\hskip 31.2982pt\mbox{ for }n=3,\end{cases}

where for
n=2
,
\nu^{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
denotes the unit vector obtained via rotating
\nu
anti-clockwise by
\pi/2
. It is clear that
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{w}\equiv\mathbf{0}
are a pair of trivial solutions to (
1.1
).
If for a certain
\omega\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, there exists a pair of nontrivial solutions
({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{n}\times H^{1}(\Omega)^{n}
to (
1.1
), then
\omega
is called an elastic transmission eigenvalue and
({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
is referred to as the corresponding pair of elastic transmission eigenfunctions.
The connection between
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{S})
and the transmission eigenvalue problem (
1.1
) can be described as follows. If
\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\infty}\equiv 0
, one clearly has
\mathbf{u}^{\mathrm{sc}}=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\overline{\Omega}
. This in turn yields that
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{i}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\overline{\Omega}
for the scattering system (
1.2
). In such a case, one can show that
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{u}^{i}|_{\Omega}
and
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{u}|_{\Omega}
are a pair of transmission eigenfunctions. However, if
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
are a pair of transmission eigenfunctions, one cannot directly have the non-scattering/invisibility unless
\mathbf{v}
can be (analytically) extended to an entire solution to (
1.1
) to generate an incident field. Nevertheless,
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
is located in any small neighbourhood of
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{S})
in the sense that
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
can respectively approximate
\mathbf{u}^{i}
and
\mathbf{u}
in
\Omega
within
\varepsilon
-accuracy for any
\varepsilon>0
such that the corresponding scattering amplitude is also of order
\varepsilon
. This viewpoint has been verified in
[
10
,
13
,
33
]
for the acoustic scattering governed by the Helmholtz equation, and one should be able to show similar results for the elastic scattering by following a similar spirit. However, this is beyond the scope the current study.

Due to its physical significance, the transmission eigenvalue problems have been extensively and intensively investigated in the literature, especially associated with the acoustic and electromagnetic scattering. It turns out that the transmission eigenvalue problems are non-elliptic and non-selfadjoint, and the corresponding mathematical study is highly challenging and intriguing, which is of significant interest in its own sake for the spectral theory of partial differential operators. We refer to
[
21
,
16
,
35
]
for historical accounts and surveys on the existing developments of the transmission eigenvalue problems. It is pointed out that the spectral study is less touched for the elastic transmission eigenvalue problems than that for the acoustic and electromagnetic transmission eigenvalue problems. Moreover, the existing results are concerned more about the spectral properties of the transmission eigenvalues and there are much fewer results on the intrinsic properties of the transmission eigenfunctions. Recently, there are considerable efforts in the literature in unveiling the distinct geometric structures of the transmission eigenfunctions.
In
[
13
]
, a local geometric structure was discovered for the acoustic transmission eigenfunctions, showing that they are generically vanishing around a corner on
\partial\Omega
. The discovery is motivated by the relevant study
[
10
,
41
,
15
]
which verified that if a scattering potential possesses a corner, it scatters every incident field non-trivially and stably. The vanishing property strongly depends on the regularity of the transmission eigenfunctions, and has been established under two regularity conditions. The first one is the Hölder continuity of the transmission eigenfunctions locally around the corner. This means that the transmission eigenfunctions should be more regular than
H^{1}
, say by the standard Sobolev embedding,
W^{1,\frac{n}{n-\alpha}}
or
W^{2,\frac{n}{2-\alpha}}
with
\alpha\in(0,1)
. On the other hand, as evidenced by the numerical study
[
7
]
, there are indeed cases where the transmission eigenfunctions are not vanishing and instead they are localizing, especially for certain concave corners. In order to gain more insightful understanding of the regularity effect, another criterion was introduced in terms of the growth rate of the Herglotz densities which arise from the Fourier extension of the transmission eigenfunctions
[
13
,
26
,
25
]
. There are several further studies on the locally vanishing property of the transmission eigenfunctions in different physical scenarios
[
6
,
8
,
12
,
14
,
17
]
. In
[
19
]
, a global rigidity property is discovered, showing that the transmission eigenfunctions tend to localize on
\partial\Omega
. Both the local and global geometric structures of transmission eigenfunctions can produce interesting and significant applications. First, they are of fundamental importance to the invisibility cloaking which is a frontier technology
[
28
,
38
]
. Second, they have been used to establish novel unique identifiability results for the inverse scattering problems by a single far-field measurement
[
6
,
8
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
17
,
18
,
26
,
36
,
37
]
, which constitutes a longstanding problem in the inverse scattering theory
[
20
,
39
]
. Furthermore, in
[
19
]
, a super-resolution wave imaging scheme was developed by making use the geometric properties of the transmission eigenfunctions.

However, most of the existing studies discussed above are concerned with the transmission eigenfunctions associated with the acoustic or electromagnetic scattering. There is very limited study on the elastic transmission eigenfunctions due to their more complicated physical and mathematical nature. In
[
8
]
, the authors proved that the elastic transmission eigenfunctions locally vanish around a corner under the condition that the eigenfunctions are Hölder continuous in the domain. The result was applied to deriving a novel uniqueness result in determining an unknown elastic source from its far-field pattern. In this paper, we shall provide a much more comprehensive study of this important geometric property for the elastic transmission eigenfunctions. First, we shall consider a more general formulation of the elastic transmission eigenvalue problem which includes (
1.1
) as a special case. Second, we establish the local vanishing property under both the Hölder continuity and the Fourier extension property of the generalised transmission eigenfunctions. Third, we apply the newly established results to deriving two novel unique identifiability results for the inverse elastic problem in determining the polygonal/polyhedral support of an inhomogeneous medium independent of its content. Finally, we would like to briefly discuss the mathematical strategies that we develop to derive the results. In order to establish the geometric property, one needs to track the singularity of the transmission eigenfunctions (with respect to their smoothness) induced by the geometric singularity of the domain, namely the corner. To achieve that end, we develop a microlocal argument that can be localized around the corner. An integral identity involving the difference of the two transmission eigenfunctions as well as a special type of CGO (Complex Geometric Optics) solutions is a critical ingredient in our study. Compared to most of the existing studies mentioned earlier, there is a boundary integral terms due to the generalized transmission conditions in our study, which together with the more complicated nature of the Lamé system, makes the related analysis and estimates highly technical and subtle. In tracking the order of the asymptotic parameter in the phase of the CGO solutions, we manage to derive the desired results.

1.2.
Mathematical setup and summary of the main findings

Let
\eta\in L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
be a real-valued function. Instead of (
1.1
), we consider the following generalized elastic transmission eigenvalue problem for
{\mathbf{v}}=(v_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{n},{\mathbf{w}}=(w_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{n}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{n}
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\lambda\Delta{\mathbf{v}}+(\lambda+\mu)\nabla\nabla\cdot{\mathbf{v}}+\omega^{2}{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{0}}}\hskip 56.9055pt\ \mbox{ in }\Omega,\\[5.0pt]
{\lambda\Delta{\mathbf{w}}+(\lambda+\mu)\nabla\nabla\cdot{\mathbf{w}}+\omega^{2}(1+V){\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{0}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\ \mbox{ in }\Omega,\\[5.0pt]
{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{v}},\quad T_{\nu}{\mathbf{v}}+\eta{\mathbf{v}}=T_{\nu}{\mathbf{w}}\hskip 93.89418pt\mbox{ on }\Gamma.\end{array}\right.

where
\Gamma\subset\partial\Omega
is an open subset. We note that if
\eta\equiv 0
and
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
, the transmission eigenvalue problem (
1.2
) is reduced to (
1.1
). Hence, we refer to (
1.2
) as the generalised transmission eigenvalue problem. It is particular to note that the transmission condition
T_{\nu}{\mathbf{v}}+\eta{\mathbf{v}}=T_{\nu}{\mathbf{w}}
not only brings mathematical generalisation but also
is physically meaningful. In fact, it is referred to as the conductive transmission condition in the context of electromagnetic scattering, which arises in effectively describing a thin layer of highly conducting coating
[
2
,
18
]
. In (
1.2
), the generalised transmission condition can also be used to effectively describe a thin layer of highly lossy elastic coating. However, we shall not explore more about this point since it is not the focus of the current article. If
\eta\equiv 0
, the existence and properties of the transmission eigenvalues to (
1.2
) have been studied in
[
4
,
5
]
. In this paper, we shall study the intrinsic geometric properties of the transmission eigenfunctions assuming their existence in the general case.

The major geometric finding can be sketched as follows. Let
\Gamma=\Gamma^{-}\cup\Gamma^{+}
, where
\Gamma^{\pm}
are two non-collinear/non-coplanar line segments or planes in 2D and 3D, respectively. That is,
\Gamma^{-}
and
\Gamma^{+}
form a (non-degenerate) corner on
\partial\Omega
. Under mild conditions on
V
and
\eta
as well as the necessary regularity requirements on
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
as discussed above, it is shown that
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
are vanishing around the corner. The 2D results are contained in Theorems
2.1
and
2.3
and Corollary
2.2
for
\eta\neq 0
and
\eta\equiv 0
, respectively, whereas the corresponding 3D results are contained in Theorem
3.1
and Corollaries
3.2
and
3.3
. According to our earlier discussion, the geometric results imply that when non-scattering or nearly non-scattering occurs, the incident and the total wave fields propagate in a peculiar manner that avoids the corner places of the inhomogeneous medium. Moreover, the results can be used to establish novel unique identifiability results the geometrical inverse elastic problem, which are contained in Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
.

Before proceeding further to prove our main result in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in Section
2
, we would like to summarize our main methodologies to provide the readers a global picture of our study. Consider the elastic transmission eigenfunctions
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
fulfilling (
1.2
). We use the elastic Herglotz wave function to approximate
\mathbf{v}
with certain accuracy and kernel increasing property, which severs as a certain regularity characterization for
\mathbf{v}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
. By virtue of the complex geometric optics (CGO) solution introduced in
[
8
]
, we establish the integral equality via the Green formula. The asymptotic decay of all integrals in the underlying integral equality with respect to the asymptotic parameter in the CGO is carefully studied, where we extract the leading order terms in the aforementioned asymptotic analysis. With the above preparations, we prove the vanishing property of the elastic transmission eigenfunction near a planar corner. For the 3D case, by using the dimensional reduction technique and similar to the 2D result, we can establish the local geometrical characterization of the elastic transmission eigenfunction near an edge corner in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
under generic conditions, which shall be clearer from our subsequent analysis in Section
3
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Sections
2
and
3
, we present the studies in two and three dimensions respectively. Section
4
is devoted to the study of the inverse elastic problem.

2.
vanishing near corners of generalized elastic transmission eigenfunctions: two-dimensional case

In this section, we consider the geometric property of the generalized elastic transmission eigenfunction to (
1.2
) in two dimensions. First, we introduce the geometric setup of our study. For
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the polar coordinate of
\mathbf{x}
is given by
\mathbf{x}=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)^{\top}
. Denote an open sector
W\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and its boundary
\Gamma^{\pm}
as follows:

(2.1)

\begin{split}W&=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|{\mathbf{x}}\neq\mathbf{0},\ \ \theta_{m}<\arg(x_{1}+{\rm i}x_{2})<\theta_{M}\},\\
\Gamma^{-}&=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|{\mathbf{x}}\neq\mathbf{0},\ \ \arg(x_{1}+{\rm i}x_{2})=\theta_{m}\},\\
\Gamma^{+}&=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|{\mathbf{x}}\neq\mathbf{0},\ \ \arg(x_{1}+{\rm i}x_{2})=\theta_{M}\},\end{split}

where
-\pi<\theta_{m}<\theta_{M}<\pi
.
Let
B_{h}
and
B_{\varepsilon}
denote open disks centered at
\mathbf{0}
of radii
h\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\varepsilon<h
, respectively. In the sequel, we set

(2.2)

S_{h}=W\cap B_{h},\,\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}=\Gamma^{\pm}\cap B_{h},\,\overline{S}_{h}=\overline{W}\cap B_{h},\,\ \mbox{and}\ \Lambda_{h}=S_{h}\cap\partial B_{h}.

The elastic Herglotz wave function
\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{g}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is defined by

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{g}}=e^{-\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{4}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\Big\{\sqrt{\frac{k_{p}}{\omega}}e^{{\rm i}k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}}g_{p}({\mathbf{d}})\mathbf{d}+\sqrt{\frac{k_{s}}{\omega}}e^{{\rm i}k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}}g_{s}({\mathbf{d}})\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\Big\}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}),

where the kernel
{\mathbf{g}}=(g_{p},g_{s})^{\top}
satisfies
g_{p},\,g_{s}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{S}^{1}})
,
{\mathbf{d}},\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
{\mathbf{d}}\perp\mathbf{d}^{\perp}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

[
3
, Theorem 3.4]

Let
D\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain with a connected complement. Then the set of elastic Herglotz wave functions is dense with respect to the
H^{1}(D)^{2}
-norm in the set of solutions to the Lamé system

(2.4)

\mathcal{L}\mathbf{v}+\omega^{2}{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{0}},\quad\mathcal{L}:=\lambda\Delta+(\lambda+\mu)\nabla\left(\nabla\cdot\right)\quad\mbox{in}\ \ D.

By virtue of Lemma
2.1
, for any generalized elastic transmission eigenfunctions
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}\times H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
to (
1.2
), there exists a sequence of elastic Herglotz wave functions
\mathbf{v}_{j}
given by

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}(\mathbf{x})=e^{-\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{4}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\Big\{\sqrt{\frac{k_{p}}{\omega}}e^{{\rm i}k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}}g_{jp}({\mathbf{d}})\mathbf{d}+\sqrt{\frac{k_{s}}{\omega}}e^{{\rm i}k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}}g_{js}({\mathbf{d}})\mathbf{d}^{\perp}\Big\}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}),

which can approximate
\mathbf{v}
to an arbitrary accuracy in
H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
. It is clear that
\mathbf{v}_{j}
can be regarded as the Fourier extension of
\mathbf{v}
.

In what follows, we shall split the real and imaginary parts of the elastic transmission eigenfunctions
({\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}})
to (
1.2
) as

(2.6)

{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{v}}_{\sf R}+{\mathrm{i}}{\mathbf{v}}_{{\sf{I}}},\quad{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{w}}_{{\sf{R}}}+{\mathrm{i}}{\mathbf{w}}_{{\sf{I}}}.

It is straightforward to verify that both
(\mathbf{v}_{\sf R},\mathbf{w}_{\sf R})
and
(\mathbf{v}_{\sf R},\mathbf{w}_{\sf R})
satisfy (
1.2
). In a similar manner, we let the real and imaginary part of the kernel functions
g_{j\beta}({\mathbf{d}})
(
\beta=p,s
) of (
2.5
) be given by

(2.7)

g_{j\beta}({\mathbf{d}})=g_{j\beta}^{{\sf{R}}}({\mathbf{d}})+\mathrm{i}g_{j\beta}^{{\sf{I}}}({\mathbf{d}}).

We can derive the following auxiliary propositions.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let the elastic Herglotz wave function
\mathbf{v}_{j}
be defined by (
2.5
). Let

(2.8)

\mathbf{v}_{j}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})+{\rm i}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{x}),

where
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})
and
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{x})
are real valued functions. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{p}}{\omega}}\bigg(\cos(k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{jp}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})+\cos(k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{jp}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})+\sin(k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{jp}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})

\displaystyle-\sin(k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{jp}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})\bigg)\mathbf{d}+\sqrt{\frac{k_{s}}{\omega}}\bigg(\cos(k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{js}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})+\cos(k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{js}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})

(2.9)

\displaystyle+\sin(k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{js}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})-\sin(k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{js}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})\bigg)\mathbf{d}^{\perp}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d})

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{x})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{p}}{\omega}}\bigg(-\cos(k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{jp}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})+\cos(k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{jp}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})+\sin(k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{jp}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})

\displaystyle+\sin(k_{p}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{jp}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})\bigg)\mathbf{d}+\sqrt{\frac{k_{s}}{\omega}}\bigg(-\cos(k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{js}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})+\cos(k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{js}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})

\displaystyle+\sin(k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{js}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})+\sin(k_{s}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x})g_{js}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})\bigg)\mathbf{d}^{\perp}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}).

Proof.

The proof follows from using Euler’s formula as well as straightforward (though a bit tedious) calculations. We skip the details.
∎

Proposition 2.2
.

Let the elastic Herglotz wave function
\mathbf{v}_{j}
be defined by (
2.5
). Denote

(2.10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{p}}{\omega}}g_{jp}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})\mathbf{d}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}),\quad\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{s}}{\omega}}g_{js}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})\mathbf{d}^{\perp}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{p}}{\omega}}g_{jp}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})\mathbf{d}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}),\quad\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{s}}{\omega}}g_{js}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})\mathbf{d}^{\perp}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}).

Then

(2.11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+{\rm i}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})

\displaystyle:=(\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0}))

\displaystyle+{\rm i}(\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})-\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})-\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})),

where

g_{j\beta}^{{\sf{R}}}({\mathbf{d}})
and
g_{j\beta}^{{\sf{I}}}({\mathbf{d}})
(
\beta=p,s
) are defined in (
2.7
). Let
J_{\ell}(t)
be the
\ell
-th Bessel function of the first kind for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
Denote
J_{\ell,\beta}=J_{\ell}(k_{\beta}|\mathbf{x}|)
,
\beta=p,\ s
. Furthermore, we have

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})=

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0,p}+\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0,p}+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0,s}+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0,s}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\Big(J_{2\ell,p}\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}+J_{2\ell,s}\mathbf{A}_{js,1}^{(\ell)}+J_{2\ell-1,p}\mathbf{A}_{jp,2}^{(\ell)}+J_{2\ell-1,s}\mathbf{A}_{js,2}^{(\ell)}\Big),

and

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{x})=

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0,p}+\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0,p}-\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0,s}-\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0,s}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}[(-1)^{\ell}\Big(J_{2\ell,p}\mathbf{A}_{jp,3}^{(\ell)}+J_{2\ell,s}\mathbf{A}_{js,3}^{(\ell)}+J_{2\ell-1,p}\mathbf{A}_{jp,2}^{(\ell)}+J_{2\ell-1,s}\mathbf{A}_{js,2}^{(\ell)}\Big),

where
\mathbf{p}_{p}=\mathbf{d}
,
\mathbf{p}_{s}=\mathbf{d}^{\perp}
, and

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{j\beta,1}^{(\ell)}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{\beta}}{\omega}}g_{j\beta}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})\cos(2\ell\theta)\mathbf{p}_{\beta}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d})+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{\beta}}{\omega}}g_{j\beta}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})\cos(2\ell\theta))\mathbf{p}_{\beta}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{j\beta,2}^{(\ell)}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{\beta}}{\omega}}g_{j\beta}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})\cos((2\ell-1)\theta)\mathbf{p}_{\beta}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d})-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{\beta}}{\omega}}g_{j\beta}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})\cos((2\ell-1)\theta)\mathbf{p}_{\beta}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{j\beta,3}^{(\ell)}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{\beta}}{\omega}}g_{j\beta}^{\sf{R}}({\mathbf{d}})\cos(2\ell\theta)\mathbf{p}_{\beta}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d})-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\sqrt{\frac{k_{\beta}}{\omega}}g_{j\beta}^{\sf{I}}({\mathbf{d}})\cos(2\ell\theta))\mathbf{p}_{\beta}{\rm d}\sigma(\mathbf{d}),\beta=p\mbox{ or }s,

and
\theta
is the angle between
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{d}
in (
2.5
). It holds that

(2.14)

\begin{split}|\mathbf{A}_{jp,i}^{(\ell)}|\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{k_{p}\pi}{\omega}}\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})},\quad|\mathbf{A}_{js,i}^{(\ell)}|\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{k_{s}\pi}{\omega}}\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})},\quad i=1,\ldots,3.\end{split}

Proof.

In view of (
2.5
), we can directly derive (
2.11
). Using the Jacobi-Anger expansion (cf.
[
20
]
), we have

(2.15)

\begin{split}e^{{\rm i}k_{\beta}\mathbf{d}\cdot\mathbf{x}}&=J_{0}(k_{\beta}|\mathbf{x}|)+2\sum^{+\infty}_{n=1}{\rm i}^{n}J_{n}(k_{\beta}|\mathbf{x}|)\cos n\theta,\\
&=J_{0}(k_{\beta}|\mathbf{x}|)+2\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}\Big[(-1)^{\ell}J_{2\ell}(k_{\beta}|\mathbf{x}|)\cos(2\ell\theta)\\
&\quad-{\rm i}(-1)^{\ell}J_{2\ell-1}(k_{\beta}|\mathbf{x}|)\cos((2\ell-1)\theta)\Big],\end{split}

where
\,\theta=\angle(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{d}),\,\beta=p,s
.
Substituting (
2.11
) and (
2.15
) into (
2.5
), we can obtain (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) by direct calculations. (
2.14
) can be obtained by using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

We shall make use of the complex geometrical optics solution (CGO)
\mathbf{u}(s\mathbf{x})
introduced in
[
8
]
, where
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is an asymptotic parameter. We next review some quantitative properties of
\mathbf{u}(s\mathbf{x})
, which shall be used in our subsequent analysis.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
8
, Proposition 3.1]

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\Omega\cap(\mathbb{R}_{-}\cup\{\mathbf{0}\})=\emptyset
. Denote

(2.16)

\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})=\begin{pmatrix}\exp(-s\sqrt{z})\\
{\rm i}\exp(-s\sqrt{z})\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(\mathbf{x})\\
u_{2}(\mathbf{x})\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})^{\top},

where
z=x_{1}+{\rm i}x_{2}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The complex square root is defined as

\sqrt{z}=\sqrt{|z|}\left(\cos\frac{\theta}{2}+{\rm i}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\right),

where
-\pi<\theta\leq\pi
is the argument of
z
. Then
{\mathbf{u}}
satisfies

\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}={\mathbf{0}}\ \ \mbox{ in }\ \ \Omega.

Let the open sector
W
be defined in (
2.1
). Then

(2.17)

\int_{W}u_{1}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}=6{\rm i}(e^{-2\theta_{M}{\rm i}}-e^{-2\theta_{m}\mathrm{i}})s^{-4}.

In addition for
\alpha,h>0
and
j\in\{1,2\}
we have the upper bounds

(2.18)

\int_{W}|u_{j}(\mathbf{x})||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\leq\frac{2(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}s^{-2\alpha-4},

and

(2.19)

\int_{W\backslash S_{h}}|u_{j}(\mathbf{x})|{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\leq\frac{6(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})}{\delta_{W}^{4}}s^{-4}e^{-\delta_{W}s\sqrt{h}/2},

where
\delta_{W}=\min_{\theta_{m}<\theta<\theta_{M}}\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)
is a positive constant.

The following lemma states the the regularity of the CGO solution
\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})
defined in (
2.16
).

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
S_{h}
be defined in (
2.2
) and
\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})
be given in (
2.16
). Then
\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})\in H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}
and
\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{0}
in
S_{h}
. Furthermore, it holds that

(2.20)

\|\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}}^{2}\leq(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})e^{-2s\sqrt{\Theta}\delta_{W}}h^{2},

and

(2.21)

\left\||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}}^{2}\leq s^{-4(\alpha+1)}\frac{4(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})}{(4\delta_{W}^{2})^{2\alpha+2}}\Gamma(4\alpha+4),

where
\Theta\in[0,h]
and
\delta_{W}
is defined in (
2.19
).

Proof.

The proof follows from a similar argument to that of
[
26
, Lemma 2.3]
and we skip the details.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose that
\Lambda_{h}
and
\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})
are defined by (
2.2
) and (
2.16
) respectively. Recall that
\delta_{W}>0
is given in (
2.19
). We have

(2.22a)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})\|_{H^{1}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{h+\frac{s^{2}}{2}}\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\delta_{W}},

(2.22b)

\displaystyle\|T_{\nu}(\mathbf{u})\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{s\mu}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\delta_{W}},

both of which decay exponentially as
s\rightarrow+\infty.

Proof.

By (
2.16
), one has

(2.23)

\|u_{1}(\mathbf{x})\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}\leq\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\delta_{W}}\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}}.

It is directly verified that

\begin{split}\frac{\partial u_{1}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial r}&=-\frac{s}{2r^{1/2}}e^{-s\sqrt{r}(\cos(\theta/2)+\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta/2))+\mathrm{i}\theta/2},\\
\frac{\partial u_{1}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial\theta}&=-\frac{\mathrm{i}s\sqrt{r}}{2}e^{-s\sqrt{r}(\cos(\theta/2)+\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta/2))+\mathrm{i}\theta/2},\end{split}

which can be used to obtain that

(2.24)

\begin{split}\frac{\partial u_{1}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial x_{1}}&=-\frac{s}{2r^{1/2}}e^{-s\sqrt{r}(\cos(\theta/2)+\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta/2))-\mathrm{i}\theta/2},\\
\frac{\partial u_{1}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial x_{2}}&=-\frac{is}{2r^{1/2}}e^{-s\sqrt{r}(\cos(\theta/2)+\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta/2))-\mathrm{i}\theta/2}.\end{split}

Therefore

(2.25)

\left\|\nabla u_{1}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}\leq\frac{s}{2}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\delta_{W}}\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}}.

Combining (
2.23
) and (
2.25
), as well as noting
u_{2}(\mathbf{x})=\mathrm{i}u_{2}(\mathbf{x})
, we can prove (
2.22a
).

Using (
1.12
), it is directly calculated that

T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}=\mu\nabla\mathbf{u}\ \nu.

Therefore by virtue of (
2.24
), (
2.25
) and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\begin{split}\|T_{\nu}(\mathbf{u})\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}^{2}&=2\mu^{2}\left\|\nabla u_{1}(\mathbf{x})\right\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{s\mu^{2}}{2}e^{-2s\sqrt{h}\delta_{W}}(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}).\end{split}

The proof is complete.
∎

We proceed to derive several key lemmas in order to establish the main geometric result of this section. It is first recalled the following Green formula for the Lamé operator; see
[
22
, Lemm 3.4]
and
[
40
, Theorem 4.4]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose that
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}(n=2,3)
is a bounded Lipschitz domain. Let
\mathbf{u_{1}}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
satisfying
\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u_{1}}\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{n}
and
\mathcal{L}\mathbf{v_{1}}\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{n}
. The the following Green identity holds

(2.26)

\int_{\Omega}(\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}-\mathcal{L}\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\partial\Omega}(T_{\mathbf{\nu}}\mathbf{u}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}-T_{\mathbf{\nu}}\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}){\rm d}\sigma.

Recall the splitting (
2.6
). In what follows, we shall mainly focus on establishing the relevant results for
(\mathbf{v}_{\sf R},\mathbf{w}_{\sf R})
. Due to the symmetric role of
(\mathbf{v}_{\sf R},\mathbf{w}_{\sf R})
and
(\mathbf{v}_{\sf I},\mathbf{w}_{\sf I})
, those results hold equally for
(\mathbf{v}_{\sf I},\mathbf{w}_{\sf I})
, and hence
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
and
\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
be a pair of generalized elastic transmission eigenfunctions to (
1.2
). Let the CGO solution
\mathbf{u}
and the elastic Herglotz wave function
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}
be defined in (
2.16
) and (
2.12
) respectively. Assume that the Lipschitz domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
contains a corner
S_{h}\Subset\Omega\cap W
, where
S_{h}
is defined in (
2.2
) and
W
is a sector defined in (
2.1
). Denote
q=1+V
, where
V
is defined in (
1.2
). Then the following integral equality holds

(2.27)

I_{1}+I_{2}=I_{\Lambda_{h}}-I_{\pm}-I_{\pm}^{\Delta},

where

\displaystyle I_{1}=\omega^{2}\int_{S_{h}}(q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x},\quad I_{2}=-\omega^{2}\int_{S_{h}}(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x},

\displaystyle I_{\Lambda_{h}}=\int_{\Lambda_{h}}(T_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}))\cdot\mathbf{u}-(T_{\nu}(\mathbf{u}))\cdot(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle I_{\pm}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}{\rm d}\sigma,\quad I_{\pm}^{\Delta}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}){\rm d}\sigma.

Here
\Lambda_{h}
and
\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}
are defined in (
2.2
).

Proof.

Recall that the differential operator
\mathcal{L}
is defined in (
2.4
). In view of the first and second equation in (
1.2
), we have

(2.28)

\mathcal{L}\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}=-\omega^{2}\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}},\quad\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}_{\sf{R}}=-\omega^{2}q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\quad\mbox{ in }\ \ S_{h}.

Using the boundary condition in (
1.2
), it yields that

(2.29)

\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}=0,\quad T_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}})=-\eta\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}\quad\mbox{ on }\ \ \Gamma_{h}^{\pm}.

Using Green’s formula (
2.26
) on the domain
S_{h}
together with
{\mathcal{L}}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}
in
S_{h}
, we have

(2.30)

\int_{S_{h}}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}))\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}\cup\Lambda_{h}}(T_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}))\cdot\mathbf{u}-(T_{\nu}(\mathbf{u}))\cdot(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}){\rm d}\sigma.

By virtue of (
2.29
), we have

(2.31)

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}(T_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}))\cdot\mathbf{u}-(T_{\nu}(\mathbf{u}))\cdot(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}){\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}-\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}{\rm d}\sigma=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}-\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}{\rm d}\sigma+\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}-\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}){\rm d}\sigma.

From (
2.28
), we have

(2.32)

\displaystyle\int_{S_{h}}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}))\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{S_{h}}(-\omega^{2}\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}+\omega^{2}q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{S_{h}}(-\omega^{2}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}+\omega^{2}q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{S_{h}}-\omega^{2}(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

By (
2.30
), (
2.31
) and (
2.32
), we can derive (
2.27
).

The proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
I_{\Lambda_{h}}
be defined in (
2.27
). Under the same setup as that in Lemma
2.6
, we have the following estimate

(2.33)

|I_{\Lambda_{h}}|\leq C\frac{\sqrt{2h+s^{2}}+\mu s}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\delta_{W}}\|\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}},

where
C
is a positive constant coming from the trace theorem,
S_{h}
and
\delta_{W}>0
are defined in (
2.2
) and (
2.19
), respectively.

Proof.

By using the Hölder inequality, Lemma
2.4
, and the trace theorem, one has

\displaystyle|I_{\Lambda_{h}}|

\displaystyle\leq\|(T_{\nu}(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}))\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}+\|T_{\nu}(\mathbf{u})\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}\|\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}

(2.34)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\|\mathbf{u}\|_{H^{1}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}+\|T_{\nu}(\mathbf{u})\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{h})^{2}}\right)\|\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}}.

Substituting (
2.22a
) and (
2.22b
) into (
2.34
), one can obtain (
2.33
).
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Under the same setup as that in Lemma
2.6
, we further suppose that the boundary parameter
\eta
of (
1.2
) satisfies
\eta\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}})
for
0<\alpha<1
. For any given constants
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
and
\gamma
satisfying
\gamma>\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}>0
, assume that there exits a sequence of
\{\mathbf{v}_{j}^{{\sf{R}}}\}_{j=1}^{+\infty}
defined by (
2.8
) with kernels
g_{jp}
and
g_{js}
can approximate
\mathbf{v}_{{\sf{R}}}
in
H^{1}(S_{h})
fulfilling

(2.35)

\|\mathbf{v}_{{\sf{R}}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}}\leq j^{-\gamma},\ \|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leq j^{\beta_{1}},\ \|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leq j^{\beta_{2}}.

Recall that
I_{2}
and
I_{\pm}^{\Delta}
are defined in (
2.27
). Then the following integral estimates hold:

(2.36)

\displaystyle\left|I_{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\omega^{2}h\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}}e^{-s\sqrt{\Theta}\delta_{W}}j^{-\gamma},

and

(2.37)

\displaystyle\left|I_{\pm}^{\Delta}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\Big(|\eta(\mathbf{0})|h\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}}e^{-s\sqrt{\Theta}\delta_{W}}

\displaystyle+\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\frac{2\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}\Gamma(4\alpha+4)}}{(2\delta_{W})^{2\alpha+2}}s^{-2(\alpha+1)}\Big)j^{-\gamma},

where
\Theta\in[0,h]
,
\delta_{W}
is defined in (
2.19
),
\theta_{m}
and
\theta_{M}
are defined in (
1.2
).

Proof.

By using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

(2.38)

\left|I_{2}\right|\leq\omega^{2}\|\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}}\|\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}}\leq\omega^{2}\|\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}}j^{-\gamma}.

In view of (
2.20
), we can immediately obtain (
2.36
).

Since
\eta\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Gamma}_{h}^{\pm})
, we have the following expansion of
\eta(\mathbf{x})
at the origin as

(2.39)

\eta(\mathbf{x})=\eta(\mathbf{0})+\delta\eta(\mathbf{x}),\mid\delta\eta(\mathbf{x})\mid\leq\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}|\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}.

By using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the trace theorem, we have

\displaystyle\left|I_{\pm}^{\Delta}\right|\leq

\displaystyle|\eta(\mathbf{0})|\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}|\mathbf{u}||(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}})|{\rm d}\sigma+\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}|\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}|\mathbf{u}||(\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}})|{\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\eta(\mathbf{0})|\|\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma_{h}^{\pm})^{2}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma_{h}^{\pm})^{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\|\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma_{h}^{\pm})^{2}}\||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Gamma_{h}^{\pm})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\eta(\mathbf{0})|\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\|\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}}\||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}}

(2.40)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\bigg(|\eta(\mathbf{0})|\|\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}}+\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}}\bigg)j^{-\gamma}.

By (
2.20
), (
2.21
) and (
2.40
), it readily yields (
2.37
).
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Under the same setup as that in Lemma
2.8
, we further suppose that
q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(S_{h})^{2}
(
0<\alpha<1
) and hence

(2.41)

\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x}):=q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\delta\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x}),\quad|\delta\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}|\leq\|\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\|_{C^{\alpha}(S_{h})^{2}}|\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}.

Then the following integral estimate holds

(2.42)

\displaystyle\left|I_{1}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\omega^{2}\Big(4\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{\pi k_{p}}{\omega}}(1+k_{p})\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})}+\sqrt{\frac{\pi k_{s}}{\omega}}(1+k_{s})\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})}\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\frac{(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}s^{-2\alpha-4}

\displaystyle+\|\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\|_{C^{\alpha}(\Omega)^{2}}\frac{2\sqrt{2}(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(4\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}s^{-2\alpha-4}+6\sqrt{2}(|\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})|+|\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}|)

\displaystyle\times|e^{-2\theta_{M}\mathrm{i}}-e^{-2\theta_{m}\mathrm{i}}|s^{-4}\Big),

where
\delta_{W}
is defined in (
2.19
),
\theta_{m}
and
\theta_{M}
are defined in (
1.2
).

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(S_{h})^{2}
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we have the following splitting

(2.43)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\delta\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x}),\quad|\delta\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}|\leq\|\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{C^{\alpha}(\Omega)^{2}}|\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}.

Therefore, it holds that

(2.44)

\displaystyle\int_{S_{h}}(q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{h}}(\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}))\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle-\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

Using the compact embedding of Hölder spaces, one can obtain that

\|\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{C^{\alpha}(S_{h})}\leq{\rm diam}(S_{h})^{1-\alpha}\|\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{C^{1}(S_{h})},

where
{\rm diam}(S_{h})
is the diameter of
S_{h}
. By direct computations, we have

(2.45)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{C^{1}(S_{h})}\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\pi k_{p}}{\omega}}(1+k_{p})(\|g_{jp}^{{\sf{R}}}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})}+\|g_{jp}^{{\sf{I}}}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})})

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{\pi k_{s}}{\omega}}(1+k_{s})(\|g_{js}^{{\sf{R}}}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})}+\|g_{js}^{{\sf{I}}}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi k_{p}}{\omega}}(1+k_{p})\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})}+\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi k_{s}}{\omega}}(1+k_{s})\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})}.

Due to (
2.18
), (
2.41
) and (
2.45
), one can verify that

(2.46)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\|_{C^{\alpha}(\Omega)^{2}}\int_{S_{h}}|\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}{\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\quad\leq

\displaystyle 4\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{\omega}}\bigg(k_{p}^{1/2}(1+k_{p})\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})}+k_{s}^{1/2}(1+k_{s})\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(S_{h})}\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\frac{(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}s^{-2\alpha-4},

and

(2.47)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\|_{C^{\alpha}(\Omega)^{2}}\int_{W}|\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}{\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\|_{C^{\alpha}(\Omega)^{2}}\frac{2\sqrt{2}(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}s^{-2\alpha-4}.

Finally, by (
2.17
), (
2.46
) and (
2.47
), one can arrive at (
2.42
).

The proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

[
26
, Lemma 2.4]

For any
\zeta>0
, if
\omega(\theta)>0
, then

(2.48)

\int_{0}^{h}r^{\zeta}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\omega(\theta)}{\rm d}r=O(s^{-2\zeta-2})\quad\mbox{as $s\rightarrow+\infty$.}

Lemma 2.11
.

[
26
, Lemma 2.8]

Recall that
\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}
and
u_{1}(\mathbf{x})
are defined in (
2.2
) and (
2.16
), respectively. We have

(2.49)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{+}}u_{1}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\sigma&=2s^{-2}\left(\mu(\theta_{M})^{-2}-\mu(\theta_{M})^{-2}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}\right.\\
&\left.\hskip 99.58464pt-\mu(\theta_{M})^{-1}s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}\right),\\
\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}u_{1}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\sigma&=2s^{-2}\left(\mu(\theta_{m})^{-2}-\mu(\theta_{m})^{-2}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}\right.\\
&\left.\hskip 99.58464pt-\mu(\theta_{m})^{-1}s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}\right),\end{split}

where
\mu(\theta)=\cos(\theta/2)+\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta/2)
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Consider the same setup as that in Lemma
2.6
and suppose that
\eta
has the expansion (
2.39
). Recall that
I_{\pm}
is defined (
2.27
) and denote

(2.50)

I_{\pm}=\mathcal{I}^{\pm}_{1}+\eta(\mathbf{0})\mathcal{I}^{\pm}_{2},

where

\mathcal{I}^{\pm}_{1}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}{\rm d}\sigma
and

\mathcal{I}^{\pm}_{2}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}{\rm d}\sigma
. Then the following estimate holds

(2.51)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm}_{1}\right|\leq O(s^{-2\alpha-2})+\left(\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}+\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\right)\times O(s^{-2\alpha-4})\ \ \mbox{as $s\rightarrow+\infty$.}

Proof.

Using (
2.12
) and the triangle inequality, one can show that

(2.52)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}^{-}_{1}|\leq|\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{11}|+|\mathcal{I}^{-(2)}_{11}|+|\mathcal{I}^{-(3)}_{11}|+|\mathcal{I}^{-(4)}_{11}|+|\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{12}|+|\mathcal{I}^{-(2)}_{12}|+|\mathcal{I}^{-(3)}_{12}|+|\mathcal{I}^{-(4)}_{12}|,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{11}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0}(k_{p}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,\ \ \mathcal{I}^{-(2)}_{11}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0}(k_{s}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(3)}_{11}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0}(k_{p}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,\ \ \mathcal{I}^{-(4)}_{11}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0}(k_{s}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{12}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell}(k_{p}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(2)}_{12}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{js,1}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell}(k_{s}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(3)}_{12}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{jp,2}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell-1}(k_{p}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(4)}_{12}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{js,2}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell-1}(k_{s}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma.

The following series expression for the Bessel function
J_{p}(t)
can be found in
[
1
]
as

(2.53)

J_{p}(t)=\frac{t^{p}}{2^{p}p!}+\frac{t^{p}}{2^{p}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}t^{2\ell}}{4^{\ell}(\ell!)^{2}},\quad\mbox{ for }p=1,\,2,\ldots,

which is uniformly and absolutely convergent with respect to
t\in[0,+\infty)
. Therefore, by (
2.53
), one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{11}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}){\rm d}\sigma+\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{(-1)^{n}k_{p}^{2n}|\mathbf{x}|^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}{\rm d}\sigma,

which can be used to derive that

(2.54)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{11}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}){\rm d}\sigma\right|+\left|\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\delta\eta\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{(-1)^{n}k_{p}^{2n}|\mathbf{x}|^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}{\rm d}\sigma\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}|\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})|\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2}\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}|\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})|\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{k_{p}^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha+2n}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r

From Lemma
2.10
, we know that

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}|\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})|\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=O(s^{-2\alpha-2}),

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}|\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})|\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{(-1)^{n}k^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha+2n}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}|\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})|\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{h^{2n-2}k^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha+2}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r=O(s^{-2\alpha-4})\quad\mbox{as $s\rightarrow+\infty$.}

Hence one can conclude that

(2.55)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{11}\right|\leq O(s^{-2\alpha-2})\quad\mbox{as $s\rightarrow+\infty$.}

Similarly, we can derive that

(2.56)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(2)}_{11}\right|\leq O(s^{-2\alpha-2}),\quad\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(3)}_{11}\right|\leq O(s^{-2\alpha-2}),\quad\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(4)}_{11}\right|\leq O(s^{-2\alpha-2}),

as
s\rightarrow+\infty
.
By virtue of (
2.14
) and (
2.53
), together with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we can derive that

(2.57)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{12}\right|\leq

\displaystyle 2\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha}\left|u_{1}\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\left(\frac{k_{p}^{2\ell}r^{2\ell}}{2^{2\ell}(2\ell)!}+\frac{k_{p}^{2\ell}r^{2\ell}}{2^{2\ell}}\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{(-1)^{n}k_{p}^{2n}r^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}\right)\right||\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}|{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\sqrt{\frac{k_{p}\pi}{\omega}}\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\Bigg(\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}\frac{k_{p}^{2\ell}h^{2\ell-2}}{2^{2\ell}(2\ell)!}\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha+2}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle+\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}\frac{k_{p}^{2\ell}h^{2\ell}}{2^{2\ell}}\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{k_{p}^{2n}h^{2(n-1)}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha+2}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-2\alpha-4})\quad\mbox{as $s\rightarrow+\infty$.}

Similarly, one can show that

(2.58)

\begin{split}&\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(2)}_{12}\right|\leq\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-2\alpha-4}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{-(3)}_{12}\right|\leq\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-2\alpha-4}),\ \ \\
&\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(4)}_{12}\right|\leq\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-2\alpha-4})\quad\mbox{as $s\rightarrow+\infty$.}\end{split}

Combining (
2.55
), (
2.56
), (
2.57
) with (
2.58
), we have

(2.59)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{-}_{1}\right|\leq O(s^{-2\alpha-2})+\left(\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}+\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\right)\times O(s^{-2\alpha-4})\quad\mbox{as $s\rightarrow+\infty$.}

Using a similar argument for (
2.51
), we can show that

(2.60)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{+}_{1}\right|\leq O(s^{-2\alpha-2})+\left(\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}+\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\right)\times O(s^{-2\alpha-4})\quad\mbox{as $s\rightarrow+\infty.$}

Finally, from (
2.59
) and (
2.60
), it yields that (
2.51
).
∎

Lemma 2.13
.

Consider the same setup as that in Lemma
2.6
and recall that
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}_{2}
is defined (
2.50
). When
s\rightarrow+\infty
, the following results hold

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{211}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{211}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{212}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{212}=\eta(\mathbf{0})\bigg(\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})\bigg)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\times 2s^{-2}\bigg(\mu(\theta_{M})^{-2}-\mu(\theta_{M})^{-2}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}-\mu(\theta_{M})^{-1}s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}

\displaystyle+\mu(\theta_{m})^{-2}-\mu(\theta_{m})^{-2}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}-\mu(\theta_{m})^{-1}s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}\bigg),

(2.61)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{212}\right|\leq O(s^{-6}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{212}\right|\leq O(s^{-6}),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\pm(\beta)}_{211}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j\beta}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}){\rm d}\sigma,\ \ \mathcal{I}^{\pm(\beta)}_{212}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j\beta}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})S_{\beta}{\rm d}\sigma,\ \ S_{\beta}=\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{(-1)^{n}k_{\beta}^{2n}|\mathbf{x}|^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}.

Denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\pm(\beta)}_{213}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j\beta}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0}){\rm d}\sigma,\ \ \mathcal{I}^{\pm(\beta)}_{214}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{j\beta}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})S_{\beta}{\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\pm(\beta)}_{22}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{j\beta,1}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell}(k_{\beta}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\pm(\beta)}_{23}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{j\beta,1}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell-1}(k_{\beta}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma.

It holds that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{214}\right|\leq O(s^{-6}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{214}\right|\leq O(s^{-6}),

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{22}\right|\leq\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{22}\right|\leq\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4}),

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{23}\right|\leq\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{23}\right|\leq\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4})

as
s\rightarrow+\infty
.

Proof.

Using (
2.12
), we can deduce that

(2.62)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-}_{2}=\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{21}+\mathcal{I}^{-(2)}_{21}+\mathcal{I}^{-(3)}_{21}+\mathcal{I}^{-(4)}_{21}+\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{22}+\mathcal{I}^{-(s)}_{22}+\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{23}+\mathcal{I}^{-(s)}_{23},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{21}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0}(k_{p}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,\ \ \mathcal{I}^{-(2)}_{21}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0}(k_{s}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(3)}_{21}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0}(k_{p}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,\ \ \mathcal{I}^{-(4)}_{21}=\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})J_{0}(k_{s}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{22}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell}(k_{p}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(s)}_{22}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{js,1}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell}(k_{s}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{23}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell-1}(k_{p}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(s)}_{23}=\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{js,1}^{(\ell)}J_{2\ell-1}(k_{s}|\mathbf{x}|){\rm d}\sigma.

Using (
2.53
), one can obtain that

(2.63)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{21}=\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{211}+\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{212}.

For
\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{211}
, we have

(2.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{211}=\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}\int_{0}^{h}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\mu(\theta_{m})}{\rm d}r,

where
\mu(\theta)=\cos\frac{\theta}{2}+{\rm i}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}
.
By virtue of (
2.64
) and Lemma
2.11
, we have

(2.65)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{211}=\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}\frac{2}{s^{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\mu(\theta_{m})^{2}}-\frac{e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}}{\mu(\theta_{m})^{2}}-s\sqrt{h}\frac{e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}}{\mu(\theta_{m})}\bigg).

Using Lemma
2.10
, one has

(2.66)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{212}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}|\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})|\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{k_{p}^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{2n}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}|\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})|\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{k_{p}^{2n}h^{2n-2}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{2}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r=O(s^{-6}),

as
s\rightarrow+\infty.

Similarly, We can derive that

(2.67)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{211}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{211}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{213}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{213}=\eta(\mathbf{0})\bigg(\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})\bigg)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\times 2s^{-2}\bigg(\mu(\theta_{M})^{-2}-\mu(\theta_{M})^{-2}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}-\mu(\theta_{M})^{-1}s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}

\displaystyle+\mu(\theta_{m})^{-2}-\mu(\theta_{m})^{-2}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}-\mu(\theta_{m})^{-1}s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}\bigg)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{212}\right|\leq O(s^{-6}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{212}\right|\leq O(s^{-6}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{213}\right|\leq O(s^{-6}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{213}\right|\leq O(s^{-6}),\ \

as
s\rightarrow+\infty.

By virtue of (
2.53
) and using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we can derive that

(2.68)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-(1)}_{22}=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}\frac{k^{2\ell}r^{2\ell}}{2^{2\ell}(2\ell!)}{\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}\frac{k^{2\ell}r^{2\ell}}{2^{2\ell}}\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{(-1)^{n}k^{2n}r^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}{\rm d}\sigma.

From Lemma
2.10
and (
2.14
) , it is easy to see that

(2.69)

\displaystyle\left|\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}\frac{k^{2\ell}r^{2\ell}}{2^{2\ell}(2\ell!)}{\rm d}\sigma\right|\leq

\displaystyle 2\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}\frac{k^{2\ell}h^{2\ell-1}}{2^{2\ell}(2\ell!)}\int_{0}^{h}re^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}|\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}|{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4})

and

\displaystyle\left|\sqrt{2}\sum^{+\infty}_{\ell=1}(-1)^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{-}}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}\frac{k^{2\ell}r^{2\ell}}{2^{2\ell}}\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{(-1)^{n}k^{2n}r^{2n}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}{\rm d}\sigma\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}\frac{k^{2\ell}r^{2\ell}}{2^{2\ell}}\sum^{\infty}_{n=1}\frac{k^{2n}h^{2(n-1)}}{4^{n}(n!)^{2}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{2}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}|\mathbf{A}_{jp,1}^{(\ell)}|{\rm d}r

(2.70)

\displaystyle\leq\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-6})

as
s\rightarrow+\infty.

Combining (
2.68
), (
2.69
) with (
2.70
), we have the estimate

(2.71)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{-(p)}_{22}\right|\leq\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4})\quad\mbox{as $s\rightarrow+\infty.$}

Using a similar argument as that for (
2.71
), we can show that

(2.72)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{+(p)}_{22}\right|\leq\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{22}\right|\leq\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4}),

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{23}\right|\leq\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4}),\ \ \left|\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{23}\right|\leq\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\times O(s^{-4}),

as
s\rightarrow+\infty.

The proof is complete.
∎

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}\times H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
be a pair of eigenfunctions to (
1.2
) associated with
\omega\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Assume that the domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
contains a corner
\Omega\cap B_{h}=\Omega\cap W
with
h\ll 1
. By rigid motions if necessary, we can assume that the vertex of the corner is
\mathbf{0}\in\partial\Omega
. Let
W
be the sector defined in (
2.1
) and
S_{h}=\Omega\cap B_{h}=\Omega\cap W
in
\Omega
. Suppose that
q\mathbf{w}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}})^{2}
with
q:=1+V
and
\eta\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}})
for
0<\alpha<1
. If the following conditions are fulfilled:

(a)

For any given constants
\gamma>\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}>0
, assume that there exits a sequence Herglotz functions
\{\mathbf{v}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
, where
\mathbf{v}_{j}
is defined in (
2.5
), can approximate
\mathbf{v}
in
H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}
fulfilling

(2.73)

\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}}\leq j^{-\gamma},\ \|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leq j^{\beta_{1}},\ \|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leq j^{\beta_{2}},

(b)

the function
\eta(\mathbf{x})
doest not vanish at the corner point, i.e.,

(2.74)

\eta(\mathbf{0})\neq 0,

(c)

the corner is non-degenerate, namely the angles
\theta_{m}
and
\theta_{M}
of the sector
W
satisfy

(2.75)

-\pi<\theta_{m}<\theta_{M}<\pi\mbox{ and }\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}\neq\pi;

then we have

(2.76)

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}|\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x})|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=0,

where
m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)
is the measure of
B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega
.

Proof.

As remarked earlier, we shall make use of the splitting (
2.6
) and it is sufficient for us to show that
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}
satisfies the geometric property (
2.76
). First, it is easy to see that
q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h})^{2}
and
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}\in H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}
can be approximated by
\{\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\}_{j=1}^{+\infty}
defined in (
2.8
) satisfying (
2.35
). Therefore the assumptions in Lemmas
2.6
–
2.13
are fulfilled.

Substituting (
2.50
), (
2.62
), (
2.63
) and (
2.67
) into (
2.27
) and rearranging the terms, we have

(2.77)

\displaystyle\eta(\mathbf{0})\bigg(\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})\bigg)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\times 2s^{-2}\bigg(\mu(\theta_{M})^{-2}-\mu(\theta_{M})^{-2}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}-\mu(\theta_{M})^{-1}s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}

\displaystyle+\mu(\theta_{m})^{-2}-\mu(\theta_{m})^{-2}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}-\mu(\theta_{m})^{-1}s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\eta(\mathbf{0})(\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{22}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{22}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{23}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{23}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{211}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(s)}_{212}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm(p)}_{214})

\displaystyle-\mathcal{I}^{\pm}_{1}+I_{\Lambda_{h}}-I_{1}-I_{2}-I_{\pm}^{\Delta}.

Multiplying
s^{2}
on the both side of (
2.77
), by virtue of (
2.33
), (
2.36
), (
2.37
), (
2.42
), (
2.50
), (
2.51
), (
2.67
) and (
2.35
), and letting
s=j^{\varrho/2}
(
\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}<\varrho<\gamma
) with
j\rightarrow+\infty
, we have

(2.78)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\eta(\mathbf{0})\bigg(\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})\bigg)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}\bigg(\mu^{-2}(\theta_{m})+\mu^{-2}(\theta_{M})\bigg)=0,

which further implies that

(2.79)

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\eta(\mathbf{0})\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})\bigg(\mu^{-2}(\theta_{m})+\mu^{-2}(\theta_{M})\bigg)=0.

Here we use the fact that

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{\sf{I}}(\mathbf{0}),

according to Proposition
2.2
.

Under the condition (
2.75
), from
[
26
, Lemma 2.10]
, we know that

(2.80)

\mu^{-2}(\theta_{m})+\mu^{-2}(\theta_{M})\neq 0.

Since
\eta
is a real valued function, by virtue of (
2.74
) and (
2.80
), from (
2.79
), one has

(2.81)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}=0.

which implies that

(2.82)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{0}.

Finally, it can be directly deduced that

(2.83)

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}|\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})|{\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}\big|\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\big|{\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle+\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}\big|\mathbf{v}^{\sf{R}}_{j}(\mathbf{x})\big|{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

Combining (
2.35
) and (
2.82
) with (
2.83
), one can prove (
2.76
).

The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 2.1
.

We would like to point out that the Fourier extension property (
2.73
) can be generalized as follows

(2.84)

\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}}\leq\phi_{1}(j),\ \|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leq\frac{1}{\phi_{2}(j)},\ \|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leq\frac{1}{\phi_{3}(j)},

where
\phi_{\ell}(j)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
are strict decreasing functions with respect to
j
and
\lim_{j\rightarrow+\infty}\phi_{\ell}(j)=0
,
\ell=1,2,3
, satisfying

(\phi_{1}(j))^{\gamma}\leq\phi_{4}(j)=\min\{\phi_{2}(j),\phi_{3}(j)\},\quad 0<\gamma<1,\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{N}.

In fact, by letting

(2.85)

s=\frac{1}{(\phi_{1}(j))^{\rho/2}},\quad\gamma<\rho<1,

one can show that

(2.86)

s^{2}\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}}\leq(\phi_{1}(j))^{1-\rho},\,s^{-2}\|g_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leq(\phi_{1}(j))^{\rho-\gamma},\,s^{-2}\|g_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leq(\phi_{1}(j))^{\rho-\gamma}

By virtue of (
2.84
), under the same setup of Theorem
2.1
, and using a similar argument as that in Lemma
2.8
, one can prove that

(2.87)

\begin{split}\left|I_{2}\right|&\leq\omega^{2}h\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}}e^{-s\sqrt{\Theta}\delta_{W}}\phi_{1}(j),\\
\left|I_{\pm}^{\Delta}\right|\leq&\Big(|\eta(\mathbf{0})|h\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}}e^{-s\sqrt{\Theta}\delta_{W}}\\
&+\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\frac{2\sqrt{\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}\Gamma(4\alpha+4)}}{(2\delta_{W})^{2\alpha+2}}s^{-2(\alpha+1)}\Big)\phi_{1}(j).\end{split}

In the integral identity (
2.77
), we can multiply
s^{2}
on both sides of (
2.77
) and choose
s
by (
2.85
). Then by virtue of (
2.86
), (
2.87
), (
2.33
), (
2.42
), (
2.50
) and (
2.51
), we can obtain

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{v}_{j}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{0}

by letting
j\rightarrow+\infty
. Therefore, we can prove (
2.76
) under the same setup of Theorem
2.1
, where the condition (
2.35
) is replaced by (
2.84
) and
q\mathbf{w}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}})^{2}
.

We next consider the degenerate case of (
1.2
) with
\eta\equiv 0
in (
1.2
). We have

Corollary 2.2
.

Under the same setup as that in Theorem
2.1
but with
\eta\equiv 0
, we have

(2.88)

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}|V(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{x})|{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=0.

Remark 2.2
.

The difference between Theorem
2.1
and Corollary
2.2
lies in their conclusions (
2.76
) and (
2.88
). If one further assumes in Corollary
2.2
that
V(\mathbf{x})\in C(\overline{S_{h}})
and
V(\mathbf{0})\neq 0
, it is easy to show that

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}|\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{x})|{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=0,

which together with the transmission condition
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{w}
on
\Gamma
further implies that (
2.76
) holds as well.

Proof of Corollary
2.2
.

The proof follows from the one of Theorem
2.1
with some necessary modifications.
Similar to (
2.27
), we have the integral identity as follows

(2.89)

I_{1}+I_{2}=I_{\Lambda_{h}},

where
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
and
I_{\Lambda_{h}}
are defined in (
2.27
). Substituting (
2.41
) and (
2.43
) into (
2.89
), one can see that

(2.90)

\displaystyle(-\omega^{2}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\omega^{2}\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}))\int_{S_{h}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}+I_{2}=

\displaystyle I_{\Lambda_{h}}+\int_{S_{h}}\omega^{2}\delta\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle-\int_{S_{h}}\omega^{2}\delta\mathbf{v}^{\sf{R}}_{j}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x},

where
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}),\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})
are defined in (
2.12
), (
2.16
) and (
2.41
).

From Lemma
2.2
, it is easy to obtain that

(2.91)

\displaystyle(-\omega^{2}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})+\omega^{2}\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}))\int_{S_{h}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{2}(\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}))\cdot\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\rm i\end{bmatrix}\left(\int_{W}{u}_{1}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}-\int_{W\backslash S_{h}}{u}_{1}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{2}(\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}))\cdot\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\rm i\end{bmatrix}\left(6{\rm i}(e^{-2\theta_{M}{\rm i}}-e^{-2\theta_{m}\mathrm{i}})s^{-4}-\int_{W\backslash S_{h}}{u}_{1}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}\right).

Substituting (
2.91
) into (
2.90
) and rearranging the terms, we have

(2.92)

\displaystyle\omega^{2}(\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}))\cdot\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\rm i\end{bmatrix}6{\rm i}(e^{-2\theta_{M}{\rm i}}-e^{-2\theta_{m}\mathrm{i}})s^{-4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-I_{2}+I_{\Lambda_{h}}+\int_{S_{h}}\omega^{2}\delta\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}-\int_{S_{h}}\omega^{2}\delta\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle+\omega^{2}(\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}))\cdot\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\rm i\end{bmatrix}\int_{W\backslash S_{h}}{u}_{1}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

Note that (
2.73
) holds. Multiplying
s^{4}
on the both sides of (
2.92
), using (
2.19
), (
2.36
), (
2.46
) and (
2.47
), and letting
s=j^{\varrho/4}
(
\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}<\varrho<\gamma
) with
j\rightarrow+\infty
, we have

(2.93)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0}).

Since

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x},

and

\mathbf{f}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{0})=\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x},

and

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap\Omega}\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}(\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x},
