Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\Omega
,
S_{h}\times(-M,M)
be described above and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. For any fixed
x_{3}^{c}\in(-M,M)
and
L>0
defined in Definition
3.1
, we suppose that
L
is sufficiently small such that
(x_{3}^{c}-L,x_{3}^{c}+L)\subset(-M,M)
and

(B_{h}\times(-M,M))\cap\Omega=S_{h}\times(-M,M),

where
B_{h}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the central ball of radius
h\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Assume that
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{3}
are the generalized elastic transmission eigenfunctions to (
1.2
), where the Lamé constant
\lambda\neq 0
. Assume further that
q\mathbf{w}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
and
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
, where
\mathbf{0}
is the vertex of
S_{h}
defined in (
2.1
). Write
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. If the following conditions are fulfilled:

(a)

\mathbf{v}
can be approximated in
H^{1}(S_{h}\times(-M,M))^{3}
by the Herglotz functions
\mathbf{v}_{j},j=1,2,...,
with kernels
\mathbf{g}_{jp}
and
\mathbf{g}_{js}
satisfying (
3.34
);

(b)

the function
\eta=\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
is independent of
x_{3}
and

(3.81)

\eta(\mathbf{0})\neq 0,

(c)

the angles
\theta_{m}
and
\theta_{M}
of the sector
S_{h}
satisfy

(3.82)

-\pi<\theta_{m}<\theta_{M}<\pi\mbox{ and }\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}\neq\pi,

then for every edge points
(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
S_{h}\times(-M,M)
, namely
{x}_{3}^{c}\in(-M,M)
, one has

(3.83)

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}),\rho)\cap\Omega)}\int_{m(B((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}),\rho)\cap\Omega)}|\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x})|{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=0,

where
m(B((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}),\rho)\cap\Omega)
is the measure of
B((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}),\rho)\cap\Omega
.

Remark 3.2
.

Similar to Remark
2.1
in the 2D case, a more general Fourier extension property (cf. (
2.84
)) can be proposed study the vanishing property
in Theorem
3.1
. However, this will involve rather lengthy and complicate analysis, and we choose not to explore more along that direction in this paper.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

As remarked earlier, it is sufficient for us to consider
\mathbf{v}_{\sf R}
and
\mathbf{w}_{\sf R}
, and prove that (
3.83
) holds for
\mathbf{v}_{\sf R}
.
Since
q\mathbf{w}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
, it is easy to see that
q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
. Similarly, we know that
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M])^{3}
under the assumption
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M])^{3}
.

Since the Herglotz functions
\mathbf{v}_{j},j=1,2,...,
defined in (
3.3
) with kernels
\mathbf{g}_{jp}
and
\mathbf{g}_{js}
can approximate
\mathbf{v}
under the condition (
3.34
), we see that
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}\in H^{1}(S_{h}\times(-M,M))^{3}
can be approximated by
\{\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\sf{R}}\}_{j=1}^{+\infty}
defined in (
3.7
) satisfying (
3.34
). Therefore the assumptions in Lemmas
3.4
–
3.12
are fulfilled.

We divide the proof into two parts.

Part I.
First we shall prove that

(3.84)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow+\infty}\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{0},

where
\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})
and
\mathbf{v}_{js}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})
are defined in (
3.27
).

By Lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
, we consider the PDE system (
3.3
). Recall that
C(\phi)
and
\mu(\theta)
are defined in (
3.51
) and (
2.49
) respectively. Substituting (
3.56
), (
3.67
) and (
3.70
) into (
3.28
), after arranging terms, we have

(3.85)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\eta(\mathbf{0})2s^{-2}\bigg(C(\phi)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})+C(\phi)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{js}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})\bigg)

\displaystyle\bigg(-s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}\mu^{-1}(\theta_{m})-e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{m})}\mu^{-2}(\theta_{m})+\mu^{-2}(\theta_{m})

\displaystyle-s\sqrt{h}e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}\mu^{-1}(\theta_{M})-e^{-s\sqrt{h}\mu(\theta_{M})}\mu^{-2}(\theta_{M})+\mu^{-2}(\theta_{M})\bigg),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\eta(\mathbf{0})(\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{23,p}+\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{23,s}+\mathcal{I}^{\pm}_{25,p}+\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{25,s}+\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{211}+\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{212}+\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{221}+\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{222})

\displaystyle-\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{1}+\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}}-\widetilde{I}_{1}-\widetilde{I}_{2}-\widetilde{I}_{\pm}^{\Delta},

where
\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{2\ell,\beta}
(
\ell=3,5
,
\beta=p,s
),
\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{21\ell}
(
\ell=1,2
),
\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{22\ell}
(
\ell=1,2
),
\mathcal{\widetilde{I}}^{\pm}_{1}
,
\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}}
,
\widetilde{I}_{\ell}
(
\ell=1,2
),
\widetilde{I}_{\pm}^{\Delta}
are defined in (
3.68
), (
3.67
), (
3.56
), (
3.29
) respectively.

Multiplying
s^{2}
on the both side of (
3.85
), by virtue of (
3.33
), (
3.36
), (
3.41
), (
3.57
) and (
3.68
), and letting
s=j^{\varrho/2}
(
\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}<\varrho<\gamma
) with
j\rightarrow+\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}C(\phi)\eta(\mathbf{0})\bigg(\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})\bigg)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}\bigg(\mu^{-2}(\theta_{m})+\mu^{-2}(\theta_{M})\bigg)=0,

Recall that the definition of
\phi
in Definition
3.1
, we know that
C(\phi)>0
. Under the condition (
3.82
), from
[
26
, Lemma 2.10]
, we know that

\mu^{-2}(\theta_{m})+\mu^{-2}(\theta_{M})\neq 0.

Under the condition (
3.81
), since
\eta
is real valued function, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\bigg(\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})\bigg)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}=0,

which readily implies (
3.84
) by noting that
\mathbf{v}_{jp}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{v}_{js}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})
is a real vector.

Part II.
By Lemma
3.13
, we have (
3.79
). Combining (
3.84
) with (
3.79
), by using the definition of
\mathbf{v}^{\sf{R}}_{j}(\mathbf{0})
in (
3.6
), we can prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{v}^{\sf{R}}_{j}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{0}.

Using a similar argument to that for (
2.83
), we can finish the proof of this theorem.
∎

We next consider the degenerate case of Theorem
3.1
with
\eta\equiv 0
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Consider the same setup in Theorem
3.1
but with
\eta\equiv 0
. If the following conditions are fulfilled:

(a)

for any given constants
\gamma>\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}>0
, there exits a sequence of Herglotz functions
\mathbf{v}_{j},j=1,2,...,
with kernels
\mathbf{g}_{jp}
and
\mathbf{g}_{js}
can approximate
\mathbf{v}
in
H^{1}(S_{h}\times(-M,M))
satisfying

(3.86)

\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h}\times(-M,M))^{3}}\leq j^{-\gamma},\|\mathbf{g}_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})^{3}}\leq j^{\beta_{1}}\ and\ \|\mathbf{g}_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})^{3}}\leq j^{\beta_{2}};

(b)

the angles
\theta_{m}
and
\theta_{M}
of the sector
W
satisfy

(3.87)

-\pi<\theta_{m}<\theta_{M}<\pi\quad and\quad\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}\neq\pi;

then for every edge points
(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
S_{h}\times(-M,M)
, namely
{x}_{3}^{c}\in(-M,M)
, one has

(3.88)

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}),\rho)\cap P(\Omega))}\int_{B((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}),\rho)\cap P(\Omega))}\mathcal{R}(V\mathbf{w})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{0},

where
P(\Omega)
is the projection set of
\Omega
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

The proof follows from the one of Theorem
3.1
with some necessary modifications. It is sufficient for us to show that (
3.88
) holds for
\mathbf{w}_{\sf R}
. Clearly, we have

(3.89)

q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}})^{2},\quad\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3},

and the condition (
3.86
) gives

\|\mathbf{v}^{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{v}^{\sf{R}}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(S_{h}\times(-M,M))^{3}}\leq j^{-\gamma},\|\mathbf{g}_{jp}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})^{3}}\leq j^{\beta_{1}}\ and\ \|\mathbf{g}_{js}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})^{3}}\leq j^{\beta_{2}},

for any given constants
\gamma>\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}>0
.

According to the proof of Theorem
3.1
, we divide the proof into two parts.

Part I.
First, let
\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)}
and
\mathbf{w}^{(1,2)}
be defined in (
3.13
), which fulfill (
3.3
) with
\eta\equiv 0
. From (
3.28
), it follows that

(3.90)

\widetilde{I}_{1}+\widetilde{I}_{2}=\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}},

where
\widetilde{I}_{1}
,
\widetilde{I}_{2}
and
\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}}
are defined in (
3.29
).
From (
2.17
), one can see that

(3.91)

\displaystyle\int_{S_{h}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\int_{W}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x^{\prime}}+\int_{W\setminus S_{h}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=6\mathrm{i}(e^{-2\theta_{M}{\rm i}}-e^{-2\theta_{m}i})s^{-4}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}+\int_{W\setminus S_{h}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x^{\prime}}.

Under the condition (
3.89
), by Lemma
3.7
, we know that
\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
,
\mathbf{f}_{3}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
and
\mathbf{f}_{4}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
have the expansion (
3.42
), where
\mathbf{f}_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
is defined in (
3.29
). Therefore
\widetilde{I}_{1}
can be rewritten as (
3.44
).

Since
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}=\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}
on
\Gamma^{\pm}_{h}\times(-M,M)
, recalling the definitions of
\mathbf{f}_{3}
and
\mathbf{f}_{4}
in (
3.29
), it is easy to see that

(3.92)

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{3}(\mathbf{0})=-\int^{L}_{-L}\phi^{\prime\prime}(x_{3})\begin{pmatrix}\lambda(v_{1}(\mathbf{0},x_{3})-w_{1}(\mathbf{0},x_{3}))\\
\lambda(v_{2}(\mathbf{0},x_{3})-w_{2}(\mathbf{0},x_{3}))\end{pmatrix}(\mathbf{0},x_{3}){\rm d}x_{3}=\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{4}(\mathbf{0})=(\lambda+\mu)\int^{L}_{-L}\phi^{\prime}(x_{3})\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}(v_{3}(\mathbf{0},x_{3})-w_{3}(\mathbf{0},x_{3}))\\
\partial_{2}(v_{3}(\mathbf{0},x_{3})-w_{3}(\mathbf{0},x_{3}))\end{pmatrix}(\mathbf{0},x_{3}){\rm d}x_{3}=\mathbf{0}.

By virtue of (
3.92
), substituting (
3.44
) and (
3.91
) into (
3.90
), we can deduce that

(3.93)

\displaystyle 6\mathrm{i}(e^{-2\theta_{M}{\rm i}}-e^{-2\theta_{m}i})s^{-4}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}\cdot(\mathbf{f}_{1j}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\widetilde{I}_{2}+\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}}-(\mathbf{f}_{1j}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{0}))\int_{W\setminus S_{h}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x^{\prime}}-\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{1j}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{4}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}.

In (
3.93
), we take

(3.94)

s=j^{\varrho/4},\quad\max\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}<\varrho<\gamma,

and multiply
j^{\varrho}
on the both sides of (
3.93
), then it yields that

(3.95)

\displaystyle 6{\rm i}(e^{-2\theta_{M}{\rm i}}-e^{-2\theta_{m}i})\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}\cdot(\mathbf{f}_{1j}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{0}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j^{\varrho}\bigg(-\widetilde{I}_{2}+\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}}-(\mathbf{f}_{1j}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{0}))\int_{W\setminus S_{h}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x^{\prime}}-\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{1j}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{4}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\bigg).

By virtue of (
3.36
) in Lemma
3.6
, we have

(3.96)

\displaystyle j^{\varrho}|-\widetilde{I}_{2}|\leq h\sqrt{2L(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})}e^{-s\sqrt{\Theta}\delta_{W}}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}j^{-\gamma+\varrho}.

Under the assumption (
3.86
), in (
3.46
), (
3.47
), (
3.48
) and (
3.49
), in view of (
3.94
), we can obtain the following estimates

(3.97)

\displaystyle j^{\varrho}\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{1j}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\omega^{2}{\rm diam}(S_{h})^{1-\alpha}4L\sqrt{\pi}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\bigg(2(1+k_{p})j^{\beta_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\varrho-\varrho}

\displaystyle+2(1+k_{s})j^{\beta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\varrho-\varrho}\bigg),\ \

\displaystyle j^{\varrho}\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{f}_{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}(S_{h})^{2}}\frac{2\sqrt{2}(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}j^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\varrho-\varrho},

\displaystyle j^{\varrho}\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{f}_{3}\|_{C^{\alpha}(S_{h})^{2}}\frac{2\sqrt{2}(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}j^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\varrho-\varrho},

\displaystyle j^{\varrho}\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{4}\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{f}_{4}\|_{C^{\alpha}(S_{h})^{2}}\frac{2\sqrt{2}(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}j^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\varrho-\varrho}.

Under the assumption (
3.87
), it is easy to see that

\left|e^{-2\theta_{M}\mathrm{i}}-e^{-2\theta_{m}\mathrm{i}}\right|=\left|1-e^{-2(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\mathrm{i}}\right|\neq 0,

since
\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}\neq\pi
.
Leting
j\rightarrow\infty
, by virtue of (
2.19
), (
3.33
), (
3.96
) and (
3.97
), we have

\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{f}_{1j}(\mathbf{0})=-\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{0}).

Since
\mathbf{f}_{1j}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=-\omega^{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
and
\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\omega^{2}\mathcal{R}(q\mathbf{w}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, we obtain that

(3.98)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})=\mathcal{R}(q\mathbf{w}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0}).

From the boundary condition in (
3.3
) for
\eta\equiv 0
, we have

(3.99)

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega))}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega)}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)}_{j})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega))}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega)}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{w}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}.

Furthermore, it yields that

(3.100)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho))\cap P(\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega)}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)}_{j})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho))\cap P(\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega)}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(q\mathbf{w}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho))\cap P(\Omega)}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega)}\mathcal{R}(q\mathbf{w}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}.

Due to (
3.98
), combining (
3.99
) with (
3.100
), we can prove that

(3.101)

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega))}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega)}\mathcal{R}(V\mathbf{w}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=0.

Part II.
Since the Lamé constant
\lambda\neq 0
, similar to Lemma
3.13
, consider the PDE system (
3.3
) for
\eta\equiv 0
, under the conditions
(
3.86
) and (
3.87
), using a similar argument of
[
26
, Corollary 3.1]
, we can prove that

(3.102)

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega))}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega)}\mathcal{R}(Vw_{3})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=0,

where
w_{3}
is the third component of
\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}
. According to (
3.101
) and (
3.102
), we finish the proof of this corollary.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

According to Corollary
3.2
, the average value of each component of the function
V\mathbf{w}
over the cylinder centered at the edge point
(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})
with the height
L
vanishes in the distribution sense. Moreover, if we assume that
V(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{3})
is continuous near the edge point
(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})
where
x_{3}^{c}\in(-M,M)
and
V(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})\neq 0
, the by the dominant convergence theorem and the definition of
\mathcal{R}
, one can show that

(3.103)

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega))}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega)}\left(\int_{x_{3}^{c}-L}^{x_{3}^{c}+L}\phi(x_{3})\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{3}){\rm d}x_{3}\right){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{0}

under the assumptions in Corollary
3.2
. Since
\mathbf{w}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{3}
, it can be readily seen that

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega))}\int_{B(\mathbf{0},\rho)\cap P(\Omega)}\mathbf{w}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{3}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{0},\quad\forall x_{3}\in(-M,M),

which also describes the vanishing property of the interior elastic transmission eigenfunctions
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
near the edge point in 3D. We would like to point out that
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
must vanish at an edger corner point
(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})
with
x_{3}^{c}\in(-M,M)
if
V
and either one of
\mathbf{v},\,\mathbf{w}
are
C^{\alpha}
smooth near the corner,
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}\in H^{2}(\Omega)^{3}
,
V((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}))\neq 0
(cf.
[
8
, Theorem 1.5]
). Indeed, the assumption that
V
and
\mathbf{w}
are
C^{\alpha}
smooth near the corner can imply
q\mathbf{w}
is
C^{\alpha}
smooth near the corner. Compared Corollary
3.2
with
[
8
, Theorem 1.5]
, we remove the assumption
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}\in H^{2}(\Omega)^{3}
and establish (
3.103
).

Similar to Corollary
3.2
, if we assume that
\mathbf{v}
has the
C^{\alpha}
-regularity near a 3D edge corner, we can use a similar argument of Corollary
3.2
to prove that
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
must vanish at the underlying edge corner point.

Corollary 3.3
.

Consider the same setup in Corollary
3.2
, but assume that
\mathbf{v}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
,
q\mathbf{w}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
and
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
, for
0<\alpha<1
. If
V(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{3})=q(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{3})-1
is continuous near the edge point
(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})
with
V(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})\neq 0
for
x_{3}^{c}\in(-M,M)
, then for every edge points
(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
S_{h}\times(-M,M)
, one has

(3.104)

\mathbf{v}((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}))=\mathbf{w}((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}))=\mathbf{0}.

Proof.

According to Corollary
3.2
, we know that (
3.101
) holds.
In what follows, let us consider two separate cases.

Case 1:
\lambda\neq 0
.
Combining the argument in
Part II
of the proof of Corollary
3.2
with a similar argument for Remark
3.3
, one can show that (
3.104
) holds by noting that
\mathbf{w}
is continuous near the edge point.

Case 2:
\lambda=0
.
Since
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}=\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}
on
\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}\times(-L,L)
, we have
\partial_{\ell}(v_{j}-w_{j})=0
for
\ell=1,2
and
j=1,2
, where
v_{j}
and
w_{j}
the
j-
th components of
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}
and
\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}
respectively. Therefore, under
\lambda=0
, subtracting the first equation of (
3.3
) from the second one of (
3.3
), it yields that

\mathcal{R}(qw_{3})=\mathcal{R}(v_{3})\mbox{ on }\Gamma_{h}^{\pm},

which can be used to further deduce that
\mathcal{R}(w_{3}(\mathbf{0}))=0
. Therefore we have
w_{3}(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})=0
for all
x_{3}^{c}\in(-L,L)
. Due to (
3.101
), we can show that
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}))=\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}))=\mathbf{0}
. Using a similar argument, we can prove (
3.104
) when
\lambda=0
.

The proof is complete.
∎

If we further require that
\mathbf{v}
is Hölder continuous at the edge corner, similar to Theorem
3.1
, we have

Theorem 3.4
.

Consider the same setup in Theorem
3.1
, but assume that
\mathbf{v}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{3}\cap C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
,
q\mathbf{w}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
and
\eta\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Gamma}_{h}^{\pm}\times[-M,M])
for
0<\alpha<1
. If
\eta
is independent of
x_{3}
and
\eta(\mathbf{0})\neq 0
, and the corner is non-degenerate, then
\mathbf{v}
vanishes at the edge point
(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
S_{h}\times(-M,M)
, where
x_{3}^{c}\in(-M,M)
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show the vanishing property for
\mathbf{v}_{\sf R}
. Clearly, one has
q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
and
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M])^{3}
. Therefore the assumptions in Lemmas
3.4
–
3.12
are fulfilled. We next divide the proof into two parts.

Part I.
Let
\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})
be defined in (
3.13
). First we shall prove

(3.105)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{0}.

From (
3.30
), (
3.31
) and (
3.32
), it follows that

(3.106)

\displaystyle\int_{S_{h}}(\mathbf{f}_{1}+\mathbf{f}_{2}+\mathbf{f}_{3}+\mathbf{f}_{4})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}}-\int_{\Gamma^{\pm}_{h}}\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma

where
\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}}
is defined in (
3.29
).

Using the conditions
q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
and
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{1,\alpha}({S_{h}}\times(-M,M))^{3}
, by Lemma
3.7
, we know that
\mathbf{f}_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h})
, where
\mathbf{f}_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
is defined in (
3.29
). Since
\eta\in C^{\alpha}\left(\overline{\Gamma}_{h}^{\pm}\right)
, we know that
\eta
,
\mathbf{f}_{2},\mathbf{f}_{3}
, and
\mathbf{f}_{4}
have the expansions (
3.35
) and (
3.42
) around the origin. Recall the definition of
\mathbf{f}_{1}
, i.e.
\mathbf{f}_{1}=-\omega^{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})
. Due to the fact that
\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h})
, we have the following expansions:

(3.107)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})+\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),\ |\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})|\leq\|\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\|_{C^{\alpha}(S_{h})^{2}}|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}|^{\alpha}.

Substituting (
3.35
), (
3.42
) and (
3.107
) into (
3.106
), we have

(3.108)

\displaystyle\bigg(-\omega^{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{3}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{4}(\mathbf{0})\bigg)\int_{S_{h}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\int_{S_{h}}\bigg(-\omega^{2}\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\delta\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\delta\mathbf{f}_{3}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\delta\mathbf{f}_{4}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\bigg)\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}}-\eta(\mathbf{0})\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma-\eta(\mathbf{0})\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle-\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma-\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma.

Combining (
2.18
) with (
2.48
) , we have the following integral estimates:

(3.109)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma\right|\leq\sqrt{2}\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r=O(s^{-2\alpha-2}),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma\right|\leq\sqrt{2}\|\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})\|_{C^{\alpha}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{\alpha}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r

(3.110)

\displaystyle\quad=O(s^{-2\alpha-2}),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma\right|\leq\sqrt{2}\|\eta\|_{C^{\alpha}}\|\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})\|_{C^{\alpha}}\int_{0}^{h}r^{2\alpha}e^{-s\sqrt{r}\cos\frac{\theta_{m}}{2}}{\rm d}r

(3.111)

\displaystyle\quad=O(s^{-4\alpha-2}),

(3.112)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right|\leq\sqrt{2}\|\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})\|_{C^{\alpha}}\int_{W}|u_{1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}{\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\sqrt{2}\|\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})\|_{C^{\alpha}}\frac{(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}s^{-2\alpha-4},

\displaystyle\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right|\leq\sqrt{2}\|\mathbf{f}_{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\int_{W}|u_{1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}{\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

(3.113)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\sqrt{2}\|\mathbf{f}_{2}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\frac{(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}s^{-2\alpha-4},

\displaystyle\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{3}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right|\leq\sqrt{2}\|\mathbf{f}_{3}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\int_{W}|u_{1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}{\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

(3.114)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\sqrt{2}\|\mathbf{f}_{3}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\frac{(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}s^{-2\alpha-4},

\displaystyle\left|\int_{S_{h}}\delta\mathbf{f}_{4}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right|\leq\sqrt{2}\|\mathbf{f}_{4}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\int_{W}|u_{1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})||\mathbf{x}|^{\alpha}{\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

(3.115)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\sqrt{2}\|\mathbf{f}_{4}\|_{C^{\alpha}}\frac{(\theta_{M}-\theta_{m})\Gamma(2\alpha+4)}{\delta_{W}^{2\alpha+4}}s^{-2\alpha-4},

as
s\rightarrow+\infty
.
From (
3.108
), after rearranging terms, we have

(3.116)

\displaystyle\eta(\mathbf{0})\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{\Lambda_{h}}-\bigg(-\omega^{2}\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{3}(\mathbf{0})+\mathbf{f}_{4}(\mathbf{0})\bigg)\int_{S_{h}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\int_{S_{h}}\bigg(-\omega^{2}\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\delta\mathbf{f}_{2}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\delta\mathbf{f}_{3}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})+\delta\mathbf{f}_{4}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\bigg)\cdot\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\eta(\mathbf{0})\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma-\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}}\delta\eta(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\cdot\delta\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}){\rm d}\sigma.

Multiplying
s^{2}
on the both side of (
3.116
), by virtue of (
2.33
), (
2.99
), (
2.100
), (
3.109
), (
3.110
), (
3.111
), (
3.112
), (
3.113
), (
3.114
) and (
3.115
), and letting
s\rightarrow\infty
, we have

(3.117)

\displaystyle\eta(\mathbf{0})\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)})(\mathbf{0})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\rm i}\end{pmatrix}\bigg(\mu(\theta_{M})^{-2}+\mu(\theta_{m})^{-2}\bigg)=0.

Since the corner is non-degenerate, we know that

\mu^{-2}(\theta_{m})+\mu^{-2}(\theta_{M})\neq 0.

Hence, we can obtain (
3.105
), which implies that
\mathbf{v}^{(1,2)}((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}))=\mathbf{0}
.

Part II.
Consider the PDE system (
3.3
) when
\lambda\neq 0
. According to
[
26
, Theorem 3.2]
, under the condition
q\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}})^{2}
and
\mathbf{v}_{\sf{R}}-\mathbf{w}_{\sf{R}}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}\times[-M,M])^{3}
we know that
v_{3}((\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c}))=0
for all
x_{3}^{c}\in(-L,L)
. For
\lambda=0
, we can use a similar argument in the proof of Corollary
3.3
for proving the 2nd case to establish the vanishing property of
v_{3}
at the edge point.

The proof is complete.
∎

In Corollary
3.5
, if global Hölder continuous regularities for
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
are fulfilled, similar to Theorem
3.4
, we can prove that
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
must vanish at the edge corner point by removing the assumption that
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}
is
C^{1,\alpha}
-continuous at the edge corner. Before Corollary
3.5
, let us recall a Schauder estimate for the Lamé operator, which is a special case of
[
27
, Theorem 5.2]
.

Lemma 3.14
.

[
9
, Proposition 2.7]

Let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. Let
\mathbf{U}\in H^{1}(D)^{3}
solve

(3.118)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{f}\mbox{ in }D,\\
\mathbf{U}-\mathbf{g}\in H^{1}_{0}(D)^{3}\end{cases}

for some
\mathbf{f}\in C^{\alpha}(D)
,
\mathbf{g}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{D})^{3}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Then it holds that
\mathbf{U}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{D})
.

Corollary 3.5
.

Consider the same setup in Theorem
3.4
. Assume that
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{3}\cap C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})^{3}
(
0<\alpha<1
) are the generalized elastic transmission eigenfunctions to (
1.2
). Suppose further that
\eta\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Gamma}_{h}^{\pm}\times[-M,M])
with
\eta(\mathbf{0})\neq 0
, and
q\mathbf{w}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
for
0<\alpha<1
. If the corner is non-degenerate, then
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
vanish at the edge point
(\mathbf{0},x_{3}^{c})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
S_{h}\times(-M,M)
, where
x_{3}^{c}\in(-M,M)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}
. It can be verified that
\mathbf{U}
solves (
3.118
) for
\mathbf{f}=\omega^{2}q\mathbf{w}-\omega^{2}\mathbf{v}
,
\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{0}
and
D=\Omega
. Due to
q\mathbf{w}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})^{3}
and
\mathbf{v}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})^{3}
, we have
\mathbf{f}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
. Hence by Lemma
3.14
, we further have
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
. Therefore the assumptions in Theorem
3.4
are fulfilled. We readily finish the proof of this corollary by Theorem
3.4
.
∎

4.
unique recovery results for the inverse elastic problem

In this section, we apply the geometric property of generalized elastic transmission eigenfunctions established in the previous sections to the study of the unique recovery for the inverse elastic problem (
1.8
).

We first introduce a more general formulation of the inverse elastic problem. Let
\Omega
,
\mathbf{u}^{i}
,
V
and
q=1+V
be those introduced in Section 1.1. Let
\eta\in L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
with
\Im\eta\geq 0
. Consider the following elastic scattering system

(4.1)

\begin{cases}{\mathcal{L}}\mathbf{u}^{-}+\omega^{2}q\mathbf{u}^{-}=\mathbf{0}&\mbox{ in }\ \Omega,\\[5.0pt]
{\mathcal{L}}\mathbf{u}^{+}+\omega^{2}\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{0}&\mbox{ in }\ \mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega,\\[5.0pt]
\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{u}^{-},\quad T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}^{+}+\eta\mathbf{u}^{+}=T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}^{-}&\mbox{ on }\ \partial\Omega,\\[5.0pt]
\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{u}^{i}+\mathbf{u}^{\rm sc}&\mbox{ in }\ \mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\Omega,\\[5.0pt]
\displaystyle{\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}r^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\left(\frac{\partial{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{\mathrm{sc}}}{\partial r}-\mathrm{i}k_{\beta}{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{\mathrm{sc}}\right)=\,\mathbf{0},}&\beta=p,s.\end{cases}

If
\eta\equiv 0
, (
4.1
) is reduced to be (
1.2
). The well-posedness of (
4.1
) for the case
\eta\equiv 0
was investigated in
[
30
,
31
]
. By following a standard variational argument in
[
30
,
31
]
, one can show the unique existence of a solution
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{-}\chi_{\Omega}+\mathbf{u}^{+}\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\overline{\Omega}}\in H_{loc}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{n}
to (
4.1
). However, it is not the focus of this article and in what follows, we always assume the well-posedness of the system (
4.1
). We write
{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{u}^{i}),\,\beta=t,p,\mbox{ or }s
to signify the far-field patterns associated with (
4.1
) and consider the following inverse problem

(4.2)

\mathcal{F}(\Omega;q,\eta)={\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{u}^{i}),\quad\beta=t,p,\mbox{ or }s,

where
\mathcal{F}
is implicitly defined by the scattering system (
4.1
). We are particularly interested in the geometrical inverse problem of recovering
\Omega
independent of
q
and
\eta
. On the other hand, it is pointed out that if
\Omega
can be recovered, the boundary parameter
\eta
can be recovered as well by a standard argument. To the best of our knowledge, the inverse problem (
4.2
) is new to the literature, in particular for the case
\eta\equiv\hskip-9.95845pt\backslash\,0
. If the far-field pattern is given associated with a single incident wave, then it is referred to as a single far-field measurement for the inverse problem (
4.2
), otherwise it is referred as multiple measurements. It can be directly verified that with a single far-field measurement, the inverse problem (
4.2
) is formally determined since both
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
(on which the far-field pattern is given) and
\partial\Omega
(it completely determines the shape of
\Omega
) are
(n-1)
-dimensional manifolds. The inverse shape problem associated with a single far-field measurement constitutes a longstanding challenging problem in the inverse scattering theory
[
21
]
, a fortiori the one described above for the elastic scattering. In the rest of the paper, we shall apply the geometric results derived in the previous sections to establish several novel unique identifiability results to the inverse shape problem (
4.2
) associated with a single far-field measurement within a certain generic (though still specific) scenario. Before that, we would like to mention in passing some related results in the literature
[
30
,
32
]
on the unique identifiability for the inverse problem (
4.2
) associated with multiple far-field measurements and
\eta\equiv 0
.

First, we introduce the admissible class of elastic scatterers in our study. Let
W_{{\mathbf{x}}_{c}}(\theta_{W})
be an open sector in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the vertex
{\mathbf{x}}_{c}
and an open angle
\theta_{W}
. Denote

(4.3)

\begin{split}\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}({\mathbf{x}}_{c})&:=\partial W_{{\mathbf{x}}_{c}}(\theta_{W})\cap B_{h}({\mathbf{x}}_{c}),\quad S_{h}(\mathbf{x}_{c}):=B_{h}(\mathbf{x}_{c})\cap W_{\mathbf{x}_{c}}(\theta_{W}),\end{split}

where
B_{h}(\mathbf{x}_{c})
is an open disk centered at
\mathbf{x}_{c}
with the radius
h\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(\Omega;q,\eta)
be an elastic scatterer. Consider the scattering problem (
4.1
) and
\mathbf{u}^{i}
is the incident wave field therein.
The scattering configuration is said to be be admissible if it fulfils the following conditions:

(a)

\Omega
is a bounded simply connected Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with a connected complement, and
q\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
,
\eta\in L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
are real valued functions.

(b)

Following the notations in Theorem
2.3
, if
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
possesses a planar corner
B_{h}({\mathbf{x}}_{c})\cap\Omega=\Omega\cap W_{{\mathbf{x}}_{c}}(\theta_{W})
where
{\mathbf{x}}_{c}
is the vertex of the sector
W_{{\mathbf{x}}_{c}}(\theta_{W})
and the open angle
\theta_{W}
of
W_{\mathbf{x}_{c}}(\theta_{W})
satisfies
\theta_{W}\in(0,\pi)
, then
q\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S}_{h}(\mathbf{x}_{c}))
and
\eta\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}(\mathbf{x}_{c}}))
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
with
q(\mathbf{x}_{c})\neq 1
and
\eta(\mathbf{x}_{c})\neq 0
, where
S_{h}(\mathbf{x}_{c})
and
\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}(\mathbf{x}_{c})
are defined in (
4.3
). Similarly, following the notations in Theorem
3.1
, if
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
possesses a 3D edge corner
(B_{h}({\mathbf{x}}_{c}^{\prime})\times(-M,M))\cap\Omega=S_{h}({\mathbf{x}}_{c}^{\prime})\times(-M,M)
, where
{\mathbf{x}}_{c}
is the vertex of
S_{h}(\mathbf{x}_{c})
contained in the sector
W_{{\mathbf{x}}_{c}}(\theta_{W})
and the open angle
\theta_{W}
of
W_{\mathbf{x}_{c}}(\theta_{W})
satisfies
\theta_{W}\in(0,2\pi)\backslash\{\pi\}
, then
q\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{c})\times[-M,M])
,
\eta\equiv 0
on
\partial\Omega
.

(c)

The total wave field
\mathbf{u}
is non-vanishing everywhere in the sense that for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(4.4)

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow+0}\frac{1}{m(B(\mathbf{x},\rho))}\int_{B(\mathbf{x},\rho)}|\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})|{\rm d}x\neq 0.

Remark 4.1
.

The assumption (
4.4
) can be fulfilled in certain generic scenario. For an illustration, let us consider a specific case by requiring the angular frequency
\omega\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
sufficiently small. In the physical scenario, this is also equivalent to requiring that the size of the scatterer, namely
\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)
is sufficiently small (compared to the operating wavelength). In such a case, from a physical point of view, the interruption of the incident field due to the scatterer should be small, i.e.
\mathbf{u}^{\rm sc}
should be small compared to
\mathbf{u}^{i}
. Hence, if
\mathbf{u}^{i}
is non-vanishing everywhere (say,
\mathbf{u}^{i}
is a plane wave, namely, the Herglotz wave (
2.3
) or (
3.2
) with the densities being delta-distributions), then
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}^{i}+\mathbf{u}^{\rm sc}
should be non-vanishing everywhere. However, a rigorous justification of such a physical intuition will cost lengthy arguments and we choose not to explore more about this point.

Next, we present a technical lemma concerning the regularity of the solution to the Lamé system around a corner (cf.
[
42
,
43
]
). We would also like to refer interested readers to
[
24
,
29
,
23
]
on classical results of decomposing solutions to elliptic PDEs in corner domains.

Lemma 4.1
.

[
42
, Theorem 2.3]

Let
\Omega
be a bounded open connected subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where the boundary
\partial\Omega
of
\Omega
is the union of a finite number of line segment
\overline{\Gamma}_{\ell}
,
\ell\in\Xi
. Fix a partition of
\Xi
into
\mathcal{D}\cup\mathcal{N}
, where
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{N}
correspond to Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions respectively. Given a vector field
\mathbf{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{2}
and
\mathbf{g}^{(\ell)}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma_{\ell})^{2}
for all
\ell\in\mathcal{N}
, consider the weak solution
\mathbf{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{2}
of the Lamé system

(4.5)

\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{f}\mbox{ in }\Omega

with mixed boundary conditions

\begin{cases}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}&\mbox{ on }\Gamma_{\ell},\quad\ell\in\mathcal{D},\\
T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{g}^{(\ell)}&\mbox{ on }\Gamma_{\ell},\quad\ell\in\mathcal{N}.\end{cases}

If
\Omega
satisfies the assumption:
\forall\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\Xi
such that
\overline{\Gamma}_{\ell_{1}}\cap\overline{\Gamma}_{\ell_{2}}\neq\emptyset
, the interior angle
\angle(\Gamma_{\ell_{1}},\Gamma_{\ell_{2}})
fulfills
\angle(\Gamma_{\ell_{1}},\Gamma_{\ell_{2}})<2\pi
and moreover, if
\ell_{1}\in\mathcal{D}
and
\ell_{2}\in\mathcal{N}
,
\angle(\Gamma_{\ell_{1}},\Gamma_{\ell_{2}})<\pi
, then a solution
\mathbf{u}
of (
4.5
) with the data
\mathbf{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{2}
and
\mathbf{g}^{(\ell)}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma_{\ell})^{2}
,
\forall\ell\in\mathcal{N}
satisfies
\mathbf{u}\in H^{3/2+\varepsilon}(\Omega)^{2}
for some
\varepsilon>0
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
S_{2h}=W\cap B_{2h}
and
\Gamma_{2h}^{\pm}=\partial S_{2h}\backslash\partial B_{2h}
, where
W
is the infinite sector defined in (
2.1
) with the opening angle
\theta_{W}\in(0,\pi)
. Suppose that
\mathbf{u}\in H^{1}(B_{2h})^{2}
satisfies

(4.6)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}^{-}+\omega^{2}q_{-}\mathbf{u}^{-}=\mathbf{0}&\mbox{ in }\ S_{2h},\\[5.0pt]
\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}^{+}+\omega^{2}\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{0}&\mbox{ in }\ B_{2h}\backslash{\overline{S_{2h}}},\\[5.0pt]
\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{u}^{-}&\mbox{ on }\ \Gamma_{2h}^{\pm},\end{cases}

where
\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{u}|_{{B_{2h}\backslash{\overline{S_{2h}}}}}
,
\mathbf{u}^{-}=\mathbf{u}|_{S_{2h}}
,
\omega
is a positive constant and
q_{-}\in L^{\infty}(S_{2h})
. Assume that
\mathbf{u}^{+}
is real analytic in
{B_{2h}\backslash{\overline{S_{2h}}}}
. There exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
\mathbf{u}^{-}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}})
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{u}^{+}
is real analytic in
B_{2h}\backslash\overline{S_{2h}}
, we let
\mathbf{w}
be the analytic extension of
\mathbf{u}^{+}|_{B_{h}\backslash\overline{S_{h}}}
in
B_{h}
. By using the transmission condition on
\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}
, one clearly has that
\mathbf{u}^{-}=\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{w}
on
\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}
. Set
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{u}^{-}-\mathbf{w}
. Set
\bm{l}_{h}
to denote the line segment with the staring point
h(\cos\theta_{m},\sin\theta_{m})\in\Gamma_{h}^{-}
and the ending point
h(\cos\theta_{M},\sin\theta_{M})\in\Gamma_{h}^{+}
. It can be directly verified that

\mathcal{L}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{f}\mbox{ in }\ \mathcal{T}_{h};\ \ T_{\nu}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{g}\mbox{ on }\ \bm{l}_{h};\ \ \mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}\mbox{ on }\ \Gamma_{h}^{\pm},

where
\mathcal{T}_{h}
is the open triangle formed by
\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}
and
\bm{l}_{h}
,
\mathbf{f}=-\omega^{2}(\mathbf{w}+q_{-}\mathbf{u}^{-})\in L^{2}(S_{h})^{2}
and
\mathbf{g}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\bm{l}_{h}})^{2}
. By virtue of Lemma
4.1
, one has that
\mathbf{v}\in H^{3/2+\varepsilon}(T_{h})^{2}
with
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Therefore by the Sobolev embedding theorem, it is clear that there exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
\mathbf{v}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}})^{2}
. Hence, we readily have that
\mathbf{u}^{-}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}})^{2}
.

The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

We would like to point out that the regularity result in Lemma
4.2
in general does not for the three dimensional case. This is mainly due to the fact that the corresponding regularity result in Lemma
4.1
is generically not true around a general polyhedral corner in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Hence, we exclude the generalized transmission condition in (
1.2
) for an admissible elastic scatterer in Definition
4.1
. That is, we only consider the case
\eta\equiv 0
on the boundary of an admissible elastic scatterer in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which is different from the two-dimensional case.

Theorem 4.1
.

Consider the elastic scattering problem (
4.1
) associated the incident elastic wave field
\mathbf{u}^{i}
and two elastic scatterers
(\Omega_{j};q,\eta_{j})
being admissible scattering configuration. Let
{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{j,\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{u}^{i})
be the far-field pattern associated with the scatterer
(\Omega_{j};q_{j},\eta_{j})
and the incident field
\mathbf{u}^{i}
,
\beta=t,\,p
or
s
. If

(4.7)

{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{1,\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{u}^{i})={{\mathbf{u}}}_{\beta}^{2,\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{u}^{i}),\ \ \hat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1},

for a fixed incident wave
\mathbf{u}^{i}
, then one has that

(4.8)

\Omega_{1}\Delta\Omega_{2}:=\big(\Omega_{1}\backslash\Omega_{2}\big)\cup\big(\Omega_{2}\backslash\Omega_{1}\big)

cannot possess a corner. Hence, if
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
are convex polygons in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or convex polyhedra in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, one must have

(4.9)

\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{2}.

Proof.

We prove (
4.8
) by contradiction. Suppose that there is a corner contained in
\Omega_{1}\Delta\Omega_{2}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that the vertex
O
of the corner
\Omega_{2}\cap W_{\mathbf{x}_{c}}(\theta_{W})
is such that
O\in\partial\Omega_{2}
and
O\notin\overline{\Omega}_{1}
. Furthermore, one may assume that in two dimensions,
O
is the origin, whereas in three dimensions, the edge corner point
O=(\mathbf{x}_{c}^{\prime},x_{3}^{c})
of the 3D edge corner
(B_{h}({\mathbf{x}}_{c}^{\prime})\times(-M,M))\cap\Omega_{2}=S_{h}({\mathbf{x}}_{c}^{\prime})\times(-M,M)
fulfils that
\mathbf{x}_{c}^{\prime}
is the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Due to (
4.7
), applying Rellich’s Theorem (see
[
31
,
34
]
), we know that
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\rm sc}=\mathbf{u}_{2}^{\rm sc}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash(\overline{\Omega}_{1}\cup\overline{\Omega}_{2})
. Thus

(4.10)

\mathbf{u}_{1}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{u}_{2}(\mathbf{x})

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash(\overline{\Omega}_{1}\cup\overline{\Omega}_{2})
. In what follows, we consider two separate cases.

Case 1 (
n=2
):
Following the notations in (
2.2
) and the setup of Theorem
2.3
, we have from (
4.10
) that

(4.11)

\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}=\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+}=\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+},\quad T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}=T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+}+\eta_{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+}=T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}+\eta_{2}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}\mbox{ on }\Gamma_{h}^{\pm},

where the superscripts
(\cdot)^{-},(\cdot)^{+}
stand for the limits taken from
\Omega_{2}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\overline{\Omega_{2}}
respectively. Moreover, we take
h\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
sufficient small such that

(4.12)

\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}+\omega^{2}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}=\mathbf{0}\mbox{ in }B_{h},\quad\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}+\omega^{2}q_{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}=\mathbf{0}\mbox{ in }S_{h}.

Since
(\Omega_{j};q_{j},\eta_{j})
,
j=1,2
, are admissible, we know that
q_{2}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}})
and
\eta_{j}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{\Gamma}_{h}^{\pm})
. Clearly
\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}\in H^{1}(S_{h})^{2}
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}
is real analytic in
{B_{2h}\backslash{\overline{S_{2h}}}}
. According to Lemma
4.2
, we know that
\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}\in C^{\alpha}(S_{h})^{2}
, which implies that
q_{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}})
. Using the admissibility condition (b) in Definition
4.1
, by (
4.14
) and applying Theorem
2.3
, and also utilizing the fact that
\mathbf{u}_{1}
is continuous at the vertex
\mathbf{0}
, we have

\mathbf{u}_{1}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{0},

which contradicts to the admissibility condition (c) in Definition
4.1
.

Case 2 (
n=3
):
Since
(\Omega_{j};q_{j},\eta_{j})
,
j=1,2
, are admissible, we know that
\eta_{2}\equiv 0
on
\partial\Omega_{2}
. Therefore, from (
4.10
), following the setup of Theorem
3.1
, it yields that

(4.13)

\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}=\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+}=\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+},\quad T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}=T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+}=T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}\mbox{ on }\Gamma_{h}^{\pm}\times(-M,M).

Moreover, we take
h\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
sufficient small such that

(4.14)

\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}+\omega^{2}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}=\mathbf{0}\mbox{ in }B_{h},\quad\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}+\omega^{2}q_{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}=\mathbf{0}\mbox{ in }S_{h}\times(-M,M).

By the well-posedness of the direct problem (
4.1
) with
\eta\equiv 0
, we know that
\mathbf{u}_{2}\in H^{1}(B_{R})^{3}
where
B_{R}
is a ball centered at the origin with the radius
R\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M]\Subset B_{R}
and
B_{R}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\overline{\Omega_{1}}
. Let
\widetilde{q}_{2}=q_{2}\chi_{\Omega_{2}}+1\chi_{B_{R}\backslash\overline{\Omega}_{2}}
. Then

\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}_{1}+\omega^{2}\mathbf{u}_{1}=\mathbf{0}\mbox{ in }B_{R},\quad\mathcal{L}\mathbf{u}_{2}+\omega^{2}\widetilde{q}_{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}=\mathbf{0}\mbox{ in }B_{R}.

Since
\widetilde{q}_{2}\in L^{\infty}(B_{R})
, by the interior elliptic regularity estimate
[
40
]
, we have
\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}\in H^{2}(B_{R^{\prime}})^{3}
, where
B_{R^{\prime}}\Subset B_{R}\backslash(\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M])
. Again using the interior elliptic regularity estimate, we have
\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}\in W^{2,4}(\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M])^{3}
. Using the Sobolev embedding theorem, we have
\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}\in C^{1,1/4}(\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M])^{3}
. Clearly,
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}
is real analytic in
\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M]
. Therefore one has
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}-\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}\in C^{1,1/4}(\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M])^{3}
. Since
(\Omega_{j};q_{j},\eta_{j})
,
j=1,2
, are admissible, we know that
q_{2}\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{S_{h}}\times[-M,M])
. Using the admissibility condition (b) in Definition
4.1
, by (
4.14
) and applying Corollary
3.3
, we have

\mathbf{u}_{1}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{0},

which contradicts to the admissibility condition (c) in Definition
4.1
.

The conclusion (
4.9
) can be immediately obtained by using the contradiction argument and (
4.8
).

The proof is complete.
∎

Based on Definition
4.1
, if we further assume that the surface parameter
\eta
is constant, we can recover
\eta
simultaneously in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
once the shape of the scatterer, namely
\Omega
is determined. However, in determining the surface conductive parameter, we need to assume that
q_{1}=q_{2}:=q
are known.

Theorem 4.2
.

Consider the elastic scattering problem (
4.1
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
associated with the incident elastic wave field
\mathbf{u}^{i}
and two elastic scatterers
(\Omega_{j};q,\eta_{j})
being admissible scattering configuration, where
\Omega_{j}=\Omega
for
j=1,2
and
\eta_{j}\neq 0
,
j=1,2
, are two constants. Let
{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{j,\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{u}^{i})
be the far-field pattern associated with the scatterer
(\Omega_{j};q_{j},\eta_{j})
and the incident field
\mathbf{u}^{i}
,
\beta=t,\,p
or
s
. Suppose that

(4.15)

{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}^{1,\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{u}^{i})={{\mathbf{u}}}_{\beta}^{2,\infty}(\hat{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{u}^{i}),\ \ \hat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{1},

for a fixed incident wave
\mathbf{u}^{i}
. Then if
\omega
is not an eigenvalue of the partial differential operator
\mathcal{L}+\omega^{2}q
in
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
, we have
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}
.

Proof.

Due to (
4.15
), we have
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}=\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\overline{\Omega}
and thus
T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}=T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+}
on
\partial\Omega
. Combining with the transmission condition in the scattering problem (
4.1
), we deduce that

\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}=\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}=\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+}=\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}\ \mbox{ on }\ \partial\Omega.

Thus, we have

T_{\nu}(\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}-\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-})=T_{\nu}(\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}-\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+})+\eta_{1}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}-\eta_{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}^{+}=(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}\ \mbox{ on }\ \partial\Omega.

Set
\mathbf{v}:=\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}-\mathbf{u}_{2}^{-}
. Then
\mathbf{v}
fulfills

(4.16)

\begin{cases}(\mathcal{L}+\omega^{2}q)\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}&\mbox{ in }\ \Omega,\\
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}&\mbox{ on }\ \partial\Omega,\\
T_{\nu}\mathbf{v}=(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}&\mbox{ on }\ \partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Since
\omega
is not an eigenvalue of the operator
\mathcal{L}+\omega^{2}q
in
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
,
one must have
\mathbf{v}=0
to (
4.16
). Substituting this into the Neumann boundary condition of (
4.16
), we know that
(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}=T_{\nu}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}
on
\partial\Omega
.

Next, we prove the uniqueness of
\eta
by contradiction. Assume that
\eta_{1}\neq\eta_{2}
. Since
(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}=\mathbf{0}
on
\partial\Omega
and
\eta_{j}
,
j=1,2
are constants, we can deduce that
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}=\mathbf{0}
on
\partial\Omega
. Then
u_{1}^{-}
satisfies

\begin{cases}(\mathcal{L}+\omega^{2}q)\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}=\mathbf{0}&\mbox{ in }\ \Omega,\\
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}=\mathbf{0}&\mbox{ on }\ \partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Similar to (
4.16
), this Dirichlet problem also only has a trivial solution
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}=\mathbf{0}
in
\Omega
due to that
\omega
is not an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}+\omega^{2}q
in
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
. Hence, we can derive
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}=\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}=0
and

T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{-}=T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}+\eta_{1}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}=T_{\nu}\mathbf{u}_{1}^{+}=\mathbf{0}\mbox{ on }\partial\Omega,

which implies that
\mathbf{u}_{1}\equiv\mathbf{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and thus
\mathbf{u}_{1}^{\rm sc}=-\mathbf{u}^{i}
. This contradicts to the fact that
\mathbf{u}^{\rm sc}_{1}
satisfies the Kupradze radiation condition.

The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

In Theorem
4.2
, it is required that
\omega
is not an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}+\omega^{2}q
in
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
. Clearly, if
q
is negative-valued in
\Omega
, this condition is obviously fulfilled. On the other hand, if
q
is positive-valued in
\Omega
, then this condition can be readily fulfilled when
\omega\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is sufficiently small.
